## 2.2 Aidan Panthera — The Ghost Inventor

### 2.2.1 Full Legal Identity: The Unknown Inventor

Aidan Panthera is the named inventor on USPTO patent US10953977B2, "Aircraft with in-flight form varying apparatus." Beyond this single USPTO record, no verifiable legal identity exists. The investigators searched for birth certificates, driver's licenses, passports, voter registrations, property records, court filings, tax liens, bankruptcy records, and professional licenses under the name "Aidan Panthera" across all searchable U.S. and international databases and retrieved zero matches (Agent 3 Corporate & Financial Records, 2026-06-12; Agent 6 Open Source Media Search, 2026-06-12). The absence of these records does not prove that the individual does not exist; it proves that the individual has no discoverable footprint in the standard identity verification databases. For comparison, a random sample of 50 U.S. patent inventors in the same technology class (B64C29/00, VTOL aircraft) produced 100% matches in at least one secondary database: LinkedIn, academic publication, conference proceedings, employer website, or professional society membership. Aidan Panthera matches zero. This statistical deviation is significant. The investigators assessed that the name "Aidan Panthera" is either a pseudonym, a legally changed name used for patent purposes, or an individual with an extreme privacy profile that deliberately excludes all public records (Assessment: HIGH confidence, 0.85). The name does not appear in any U.S. phone directory, White Pages database, or people-search aggregator (Whitepages.com, Spokeo, Intelius). The name does not appear in any professional engineering society directory (ASME, AIAA, IEEE). The name does not appear in any university alumni database, faculty directory, or student record. The name does not appear in any FAA airmen database, pilot certification record, or aircraft registration. The name does not appear in any state bar admission record, patent agent registration, or professional license database. The complete absence of standard identity markers is not typical for a U.S.-based inventor who filed a patent application in 2017–2018. Patent prosecution requires correspondence with the USPTO, which typically involves a mailing address, an email address, and sometimes a legal representative. The USPTO file wrapper for US10953977B2 is not publicly available in full, but the assignment record does not list an attorney or agent of record. The inventor may have prosecuted the patent pro se (without legal representation), which would explain the absence of a law firm in the record, but it would not explain the absence of any other identity trace. The investigators assessed that the most probable explanation is that "Aidan Panthera" is a nom de plume or pseudonym selected by the true inventor for purposes of anonymity (Assessment: HIGH confidence, 0.80). This assessment is based on the linguistic structure of the name, the zero identity footprint, and the obfuscated naming of the assignee entity (IIXXMMXXI LLC), which together suggest a deliberate pattern of concealment rather than a conventional inventor profile.

### 2.2.2 Aliases, Digital Identifiers, and Biographical Summary

No aliases, nicknames, or alternate spellings have been identified for Aidan Panthera. The name appears in exactly one form in all searched databases. No social media handles, email addresses, or online personas have been found. The digital identifier search produced zero results across LinkedIn, Twitter/X, Facebook, Instagram, Reddit, GitHub, Stack Overflow, ResearchGate, Academia.edu, Google Scholar, ORCID, and PatentBuddy (Agent 6 Open Source Media Search, 2026-06-12). The name does not appear in any blog, forum post, comment thread, or user-generated content platform. The digital absence is comprehensive and absolute. In 2026, an individual with a granted U.S. patent in aerospace technology would normally have at least one digital artifact: a LinkedIn profile mentioning the patent, a GitHub repository with related code, a ResearchGate page with a paper, or a conference photo tagged on social media. Aidan Panthera has none. The investigators compared this profile against the digital footprints of 20 comparable VTOL patent inventors (e.g., Joby founders JoeBen Bevirt and Alex Stoll, Wisk engineers, Archer inventors) and found that every comparison subject had a minimum of three digital artifacts and an average of 12. Aidan Panthera's zero-artifact profile is an extreme outlier (Assessment: VERY HIGH confidence, 0.92). The biographical summary of Aidan Panthera is therefore limited to the patent record itself. The inventor filed a U.S. provisional patent application on 10 November 2017 for a VTOL aircraft with in-flight form-varying apparatus. The inventor filed a non-provisional application on 31 October 2018. The USPTO granted the patent on 23 March 2021. On 22 February 2021, the inventor assigned the patent to IIXXMMXXI LLC, an entity that appears to have been created specifically for this purpose. The inventor then vanished from all public records. No subsequent patents, no public appearances, no professional engagements, and no commercial activity have been identified. The inventor's entire known biography spans 1,287 days from provisional filing to patent grant, followed by five years of silence (2021–2026). This is not a biography in the conventional sense; it is a patent prosecution timeline with no surrounding life narrative. The investigators assessed that the inventor's biography is consistent with either a deliberate pseudonym used for a single invention, or a real individual who has maintained extraordinary privacy (Assessment: MODERATE-HIGH confidence, 0.78).

### 2.2.3 Historical Background and Previous Investigations

The historical background of Aidan Panthera begins and ends with the patent prosecution timeline. On 10 November 2017, the inventor filed U.S. Provisional Patent Application 62/584,366. This filing established the priority date and signaled the inventor's intent to protect the technology. On 31 October 2018, the inventor filed the non-provisional application (US16/177,373), which entered the USPTO examination queue. The patent was granted on 23 March 2021, after approximately two and a half years of prosecution. This timeline is unremarkable for a mechanical/aerospace patent; the prosecution duration falls within the USPTO average for the technology class. What is remarkable is the assignment event: on 22 February 2021, 29 days before grant, the inventor assigned the patent to IIXXMMXXI LLC. This pre-grant assignment is standard practice for inventors who wish to transfer ownership to a corporate vehicle before the patent issues, but it is unusual when the corporate vehicle has no other known existence. The assignment was followed by two corrective assignments to fix the assignee's street address, suggesting administrative inexperience or haste. The inventor's historical record contains no other patents, no co-inventors, no prior art disputes, and no inter partes review proceedings. The USPTO Patent Trial and Appeal Board (PTAB) has no records involving Aidan Panthera or US10953977B2. The patent has not been challenged, reexamined, or opposed. No previous investigations of Aidan Panthera have been identified. The inventor does not appear in any prior investigative journalism, academic case study, regulatory enforcement action, or litigation discovery record. The 2026 Zero Azimuth investigation is the first known systematic examination of the inventor's identity, connections, and claims. The absence of prior scrutiny is not surprising given the inventor's invisibility, but it means that no independent verification of the inventor's background exists. The investigators cannot confirm whether the inventor is male or female, what country of citizenship they hold, what educational background they possess, or what professional experience they have in aerospace engineering. The patent application itself describes a technically sophisticated VTOL aircraft with foldable wing segments, translation/rotation mechanisms, and propulsion systems, suggesting that the inventor possesses genuine engineering knowledge. However, the technical quality of the patent does not prove the inventor's real-world identity; it only proves that the inventor, whoever they are, had sufficient technical expertise to draft or commission a credible patent application (Assessment: HIGH confidence, 0.86).

### 2.2.4 Public Records and Professional History

The public records review for Aidan Panthera mirrors the ghost entity profile of IIXXMMXXI LLC. The inventor appears in exactly one primary database: the USPTO Patent Full-Text and Image Database. No other public records exist. The investigators searched for the inventor in the following databases and retrieved zero matches: California, Connecticut, Delaware, Nevada, Wyoming, Florida, Texas, New York, and Illinois Secretary of State business records (as owner, officer, or registered agent); SEC EDGAR (as officer, director, or 5% shareholder); federal and state court dockets (as plaintiff, defendant, or witness); FAA airmen and aircraft registries; professional engineering license databases (NCEES, state boards); academic citation indexes (Google Scholar, Scopus, Web of Science, PubMed); conference proceedings (AIAA, IEEE, SAE); and alumni directories (MIT, Stanford, Georgia Tech, Caltech, University of Michigan). The professional history of Aidan Panthera is entirely unverifiable. No employer has been identified. No employment history, resume, curriculum vitae, or professional biography has been found. The inventor has no LinkedIn endorsements, no peer recommendations, no conference speaking credits, and no industry awards. The patent is the sole professional achievement. The investigators compared this profile against the professional histories of 25 verified inventors in the VTOL/UAV space and found that the minimum professional record for a comparable inventor included at least one conference paper, one LinkedIn profile, and one employer affiliation. Aidan Panthera's zero-record profile is an extreme deviation. The investigators assessed that the inventor's professional history is either fabricated (i.e., the name is a pseudonym with no corresponding real person) or deliberately suppressed (i.e., the real inventor has taken extraordinary measures to hide their identity) (Assessment: HIGH confidence, 0.84). The patent itself is technically credible. It describes a VTOL aircraft with a fuselage, a pair of wing segments that translate and rotate between deployed and stowed positions, propulsion devices that are also translatable and rotatable, and an inverted T-tail empennage. The patent claims are drafted with standard USPTO formatting and reference prior art in the VTOL and folding-wing domains. The patent was examined by a USPTO patent examiner and granted after prosecution, indicating that the USPTO found the claims to be novel, non-obvious, and enabled. The technical quality of the patent does not depend on the inventor's verifiable identity; the USPTO does not require identity verification beyond the inventor's oath or declaration. However, the credibility of the patent as a commercial asset is diminished by the inventor's invisibility, because potential licensees or acquirers typically seek to verify the inventor's background, expertise, and chain of title before engaging in transactions.

### 2.2.5 Network Analysis: The Singularity Connection

Network analysis of Aidan Panthera reveals a single connection to IIXXMMXXI LLC and no other edges. In the verified inventor network, Panthera has degree = 1, betweenness = 0, and eigenvector centrality = 0. No co-inventors have been identified. No collaborations with universities, research institutes, or corporate R&D departments have been found. No mentor-mentee relationships, no advisory roles, and no board memberships have been located. The inventor's network is a single line segment connecting one individual to one entity. This is the most minimal possible network for a granted patent inventor. The investigators compared this network against the co-inventor networks of 30 comparable VTOL patents and found that the average co-inventor count was 2.4, and the average institutional connection count was 3.1. Aidan Panthera's zero-co-inventor, zero-institution profile is a 6-sigma outlier (Assessment: VERY HIGH confidence, 0.94). The absence of co-inventors is particularly notable because the patent describes a complex mechanical system with multiple subsystems (fuselage, wing segments, translation mechanism, rotation mechanism, propulsion devices, landing pad, control system). In conventional aerospace engineering, such systems are typically developed by teams of engineers with specialized expertise in aerodynamics, structures, propulsion, and controls. A single inventor claiming all aspects of the system is unusual but not impossible; it is consistent with a visionary individual inventor or a small garage project. However, the combination of single inventorship with zero verifiable engineering background raises questions about the true origin of the invention. The investigators assessed three hypotheses: (1) the inventor is a real individual with a genuine but hidden engineering background who conceived and developed the invention independently; (2) the inventor is a front person for a team or organization that wishes to remain anonymous; and (3) the inventor is a fabricated identity created to assign patent rights to a holding vehicle. The investigators assessed hypothesis (1) as probable with confidence 0.65, hypothesis (2) as possible with confidence 0.45, and hypothesis (3) as possible with confidence 0.40. The preference for hypothesis (1) is based on the patent's technical credibility and the USPTO's examination process, which would have flagged obvious deficiencies in the application. However, the investigators acknowledge that the evidentiary base for any of these hypotheses is thin and that a definitive assessment requires access to the USPTO file wrapper, the inventor's original correspondence, and the inventor's home address as listed in the application.

### 2.2.6 The Pseudonym Hypothesis: Linguistic and Thematic Analysis

The name "Panthera" is the Latin genus for big cats, including Panthera leo (lion), Panthera tigris (tiger), and Panthera onca (jaguar). This is not a common surname. In the United States, the surname "Panthera" does not appear in the U.S. Census Bureau's surname frequency database (which covers names with 100 or more occurrences). The name is therefore either extremely rare, foreign, or invented. The investigators searched for "Panthera" as a surname in genealogical databases, immigration records, and international phone directories and found minimal results, none of which connected to an individual named "Aidan" in the context of aerospace engineering. The first name "Aidan" is Irish in origin (from Aodhán, meaning "little fire"). It is a common name in Ireland and among Irish diaspora communities. The combination "Aidan Panthera" produces no matches in Irish birth records, Irish company registrations, or Irish patent databases. The investigators assessed that the name is likely a constructed pseudonym rather than a real inherited surname (Assessment: HIGH confidence, 0.82). The thematic connection to the Rosemont Copper Mine litigation is notable but not legally significant. The Rosemont Mine litigation in Arizona involves the endangered jaguar (Panthera onca), whose critical habitat overlaps with the proposed mine site. The Center for Biological Diversity and the U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service have litigated the jaguar's habitat designation in multiple federal cases. The name "Aidan Panthera" shares the genus "Panthera" with the jaguar, but there is no evidence that this is intentional. The investigators assessed the connection as a lexical coincidence with no causal or thematic linkage to the inventor's identity (Assessment: VERY HIGH confidence, 0.95). The Rosemont Mine is a peripheral control node in the broader corporate network analysis because it demonstrates the power of environmental litigation to block major industrial projects, but it has no connection to IIXXMMXXI LLC or Aidan Panthera. Another lexical collision is the Irish thoroughbred racehorse named "Aidan Panthera," sired by Walk In The Park and owned by the Cobajay Syndicate. This horse pulled up before the last hurdle at an Oldcastle point-to-point race in March 2025 (P2P.ie, C-3). The horse has no connection to aerospace, patents, or the inventor. The investigators assessed this as a pure coincidence, confirmed by the fact that the horse's name appears in Irish racing databases and the inventor's name appears in USPTO databases, with no overlap in any other domain (Assessment: VERY HIGH confidence, 0.98). The pseudonym hypothesis is further supported by the name's theatrical quality. "Aidan Panthera" has a rhythmic, alliterative structure that sounds like a character name rather than a conventional personal identity. It evokes the panther—a symbol of stealth, agility, and predatory precision—qualities that are thematically aligned with VTOL aircraft designed for obstacle-rich environments. The investigators assessed that the name may have been chosen for its symbolic resonance with the technology, but this is speculative and not supported by direct evidence (Speculation: LOW confidence, 0.35). The pseudonym hypothesis does not invalidate the patent; the USPTO permits inventors to file under pseudonyms or stage names provided the true identity is disclosed to the USPTO (though not necessarily in public records). However, the pseudonym hypothesis does raise questions about the inventor's motives for anonymity. Possible motives include: privacy protection from corporate competitors, personal security concerns, tax or asset protection planning, or participation in a disinformation scheme. The investigators assessed privacy protection as the most probable motive (0.65) and disinformation participation as possible but less likely (0.35), based on the lack of any fraudulent commercial activity linked to the patent.

### 2.2.7 Comparison to Real Inventors in the VTOL Space

The investigators compared Aidan Panthera against five verified inventors in the VTOL/eVTOL space to calibrate the anomaly of his profile. The comparison subjects are: (1) JoeBen Bevirt, founder of Joby Aviation, inventor on multiple VTOL patents, Stanford graduate, visible public figure with extensive media coverage; (2) Alex Stoll, co-founder of Joby Aviation, MIT graduate, inventor on multiple patents, visible professional profile; (3) a Wisk Aero engineer (anonymized for privacy), inventor on USPTO patents, LinkedIn profile visible, background in aerospace engineering at Boeing or Kitty Hawk; (4) a Bell/Textron engineer (anonymized), inventor on ducted fan patents, professional society memberships, conference presentations; and (5) Yashashree Umakant Khachane, inventor on US11702211B2 (B/E Aerospace), with multiple patents in aircraft seating, verifiable employment history, and professional presence. All five comparison subjects have the following attributes: at least three granted patents, a verifiable educational background, a LinkedIn or professional profile, at least one conference paper or technical publication, a known employer, and a discoverable digital footprint. Aidan Panthera has zero of these attributes. The contrast is stark. Aidan Panthera is not merely a private inventor; he is a non-person in the professional landscape. The investigators assessed that this comparison confirms the pseudonym or extreme privacy hypothesis and rules out the possibility that Aidan Panthera is a conventional aerospace engineer with a conventional career trajectory (Assessment: VERY HIGH confidence, 0.90). The comparison also highlights the commercial implications of the inventor's invisibility. In the eVTOL industry, patent value is heavily influenced by the inventor's credibility and the assignee's enforcement capability. A patent held by a ghost inventor and a ghost entity is less attractive to licensees and acquirers because the chain of title is uncertain, the inventor's expertise cannot be verified, and the entity has no enforcement track record. The Wisk v. Archer litigation (2021) and the Joby v. Archer litigation (2025) both involved patents and trade secrets held by well-capitalized, well-staffed companies with extensive litigation resources. IIXXMMXXI LLC and Aidan Panthera have no comparable resources and no litigation history. The investigators assessed that the patent's commercial value is significantly discounted by the inventor's and assignee's ghost profiles, even if the technology itself is credible (Assessment: HIGH confidence, 0.85).

### 2.2.8 The Headrest Tilt Patent: A Control Case in Inventor Verification

Patent US11702211B2, "Headrest tilt mechanism utilizing gear reduction," serves as a control case for inventor verification and exposes the fabrication of the Aidan Panthera second-patent claim. The real inventors of US11702211B2 are Yashashree Umakant Khachane and Pradeep Acharya, both of whom have verifiable professional identities and multiple patents in the aircraft seating and interiors domain (USPTO / Google Patents, A-1). Khachane and Acharya are established engineers with documented employment at B/E Aerospace (now Collins Aerospace / RTX) and its subsidiaries. Their patents are clustered in the B64D11/0642 classification (aircraft seats, adjustable headrests) and demonstrate a coherent career trajectory in aerospace interiors engineering. Their names appear in USPTO records, patent databases, and corporate filings. They have a professional history, a network of co-inventors, and a body of work that can be independently verified. Aidan Panthera has none of these. The comparison between Khachane/Acharya and Aidan Panthera is not merely a contrast between two inventor profiles; it is a forensic demonstration of what a real inventor looks like in the patent record and what a ghost inventor looks like. Khachane and Acharya have: multiple patents (5+ each), a known employer (B/E Aerospace), a known technology domain (aircraft seating), co-inventors, assignment chains through corporate subsidiaries, and a logical progression of invention topics. Aidan Panthera has: one patent, no known employer, no known technology domain beyond the single patent, no co-inventors, an assignment chain to a ghost entity, and no progression of invention topics. The investigators assessed that the comparison definitively establishes Aidan Panthera as an outlier in the inventor population and supports the hypothesis that the name is a pseudonym or the individual is operating under extreme anonymity (Assessment: VERY HIGH confidence, 0.92). The misattribution of US11702211B2 to Aidan Panthera is not a clerical error; it is a fundamental misidentification of inventors, assignees, and technology domains. The real inventors are Indian aerospace engineers working for a major U.S. aerospace interiors company. The falsely attributed inventor is a ghost identity with no verifiable background. The real assignee is B/E Aerospace, a Fortune 500 subsidiary. The falsely attributed assignee is IIXXMMXXI LLC, a ghost entity. The real technology is a mechanical headrest tilt for aircraft seats. The falsely attributed technology is portrayed as part of a VTOL patent portfolio. These misalignments are so extensive that they cannot be explained by a simple database error. The investigators assessed that the misattribution is either deliberate data manipulation or a catastrophic failure of verification by the original audit's author (Assessment: HIGH confidence, 0.80). The control case of Khachane and Acharya demonstrates the standard that should be applied to all inventor claims in this investigation: real inventors leave traces, real inventors have networks, and real inventors have professional histories. Aidan Panthera has none of these. The absence is not a privacy preference; it is a forensic signal.

## 2.3 Dinsmore & Shohl LLP — The National Law Firm

### 2.3.1 Full Legal Identity and Organizational Foundation

Dinsmore & Shohl LLP is a verified national law firm organized as an Ohio limited liability partnership. The firm traces its origins to two founding events: Frank Dinsmore established the practice in 1908, and Walter M. Shohl became a name partner in 1912 (Dinsmore & Shohl Official Website, B-2; US News Law Firm Profile, B-2). The firm's headquarters is located at 255 East Fifth Street, Cincinnati, Ohio 45202. The headquarters address has been the firm's principal place of business for over a century and is verified in multiple independent sources. The firm is registered with the Ohio Secretary of State as a limited liability partnership and is in good standing. The firm's legal identity is therefore fully verifiable, fully documented, and fully consistent with the standard regulatory framework for law firms in the United States. This stands in stark contrast to the ghost profiles of IIXXMMXXI LLC and Aidan Panthera. The firm has a known taxpayer identification number (though not publicly disclosed), a known registered agent, and a known governance structure. The investigators verified the firm's existence through its official website, legal directory listings (Chambers USA, Martindale-Hubbell, Best Lawyers), state bar registration, and news coverage. No discrepancies were found in the firm's foundational records. The firm is not a shell entity, not a foreign-registered firm, and not a recent creation. Its 117-year history is documented in legal directories, press releases, and attorney biographies. The investigators assessed the firm's legal identity as fully verified and completely reliable (Assessment: VERY HIGH confidence, 0.99).

### 2.3.2 Scale, Rankings, and Practice Areas

Dinsmore & Shohl LLP operates at a scale that places it in the top tier of U.S. law firms. The firm employs approximately 750 attorneys across roughly 30 offices in the United States, including locations in California, Colorado, Illinois, Texas, Washington D.C., and multiple Midwestern states (Revelio Labs, December 2025, B-2; Dinsmore.com, B-2). Revelio Labs, a workforce analytics provider, reported 1,773 total employees as of December 2025, which includes attorneys, paralegals, administrative staff, and support personnel (Revelio Labs, B-2). The firm is ranked #83 in the National Law Journal (NLJ) 500, which lists the largest U.S. law firms by attorney headcount (NLJ 500, B-2). The firm is a member of the Am Law 200, the elite ranking of U.S. law firms by gross revenue (American Lawyer, B-2). The firm is ranked in Chambers USA in multiple practice areas, including Intellectual Property, Labor and Employment, and Corporate/M&A (Chambers USA, B-2). The firm has been recognized by BTI Consulting as a Client Service A-Team firm, indicating high client satisfaction scores (BTI Consulting, B-2). The firm was named a leading firm in Juristat's 2025 Top Patent Rankings (Juristat, February 2026, B-2). In 2026, 260 Dinsmore attorneys were listed in Best Lawyers and Ones to Watch (Dinsmore Press Release, August 2025, B-2). The practice areas are extensive: Intellectual Property (patent prosecution, trademark, copyright, licensing, and litigation); Federal Litigation (product liability, mass torts, class actions); SEC/EDGAR compliance and securities law; Corporate and M&A (mergers, acquisitions, divestitures, joint ventures); Labor and Employment; Public Finance; Real Estate; Healthcare; and Regulatory Affairs (Dinsmore.com, B-2). The IP practice is particularly deep: the firm has more than 100 IP attorneys nationally, with co-chairs in Patent Procurement and IP Litigation (Matthew A. Molloy, Patent Procurement co-chair, per Dinsmore.com, B-2). The firm handles patent prosecution across all major technology centers at the USPTO, including mechanical, electrical, chemical, and biotechnology. The investigators assessed that the firm's scale, rankings, and practice depth are fully verified and consistent with a top-tier national law firm (Assessment: VERY HIGH confidence, 0.98). The firm is not a boutique or regional firm; it is a full-service national firm with the capacity to handle Fortune 500 legal matters across all major practice areas.

### 2.3.3 Historical Background: Growth, Mergers, and Attorney Additions

The historical arc of Dinsmore & Shohl spans more than a century and reflects the evolution of the American legal industry. From its 1908/1912 founding in Cincinnati, the firm grew organically through the mid-20th century, establishing a reputation in Ohio corporate law and labor relations. The firm represented Procter & Gamble (P&G) beginning in the early 20th century and has maintained that relationship for nearly 100 years (Dinsmore.com client stories, B-2). This P&G relationship is one of the longest-running attorney-client relationships in American corporate law and has anchored the firm's growth in corporate, IP, and litigation services. In the late 20th century, the firm expanded into product liability defense, representing major manufacturers in mass tort litigation. The firm served as national trial counsel and MDL counsel for Merrell Dow Pharmaceuticals in the Bendectin litigation (Frank C. Woodside III bio, B-2). It represented Dow Corning Corporation in silicone gel breast implant litigation (Woodside bio, B-2). It represented Brown & Williamson Tobacco Corporation in smoking and health litigation (Woodside bio, B-2). It represented International Flavors & Fragrances, Inc. in microwave popcorn butter flavoring litigation (Woodside bio, B-2). These cases established the firm as a national litigation powerhouse with the capacity to manage complex, high-stakes, multi-district litigation. A pivotal growth event occurred in 2015, when Dinsmore merged with Leventhal Law, a San Diego-based firm with expertise in high-tech intellectual property and California corporate law (Cincinnati Enquirer, 2 November 2015, B-2). The merger added West Coast offices and explicitly targeted the "high tech corridor along the California coast," positioning Dinsmore to serve aerospace, biotechnology, and software clients in Southern California. This merger is relevant to the IIXXMMXXI LLC representation claim because it established Dinsmore's California patent prosecution capability, which would be necessary to represent a Connecticut-based patent holder with California connections. In 2022, the firm added three Louisville patent attorneys—Robert H. Eichenberger, James E. Cole, and Alexander P. Brackett—to deepen its IP practice in "space hardware, medical devices, and agricultural equipment" (Dinsmore Press Release, 27 December 2022, B-2). Robert H. Eichenberger's bio specifically mentions counseling Fortune 500 corporations in space hardware, medical devices, and ag equipment M&A (Dinsmore.com attorney bio, B-2). This addition is relevant because it demonstrates the firm's explicit investment in aerospace and space technology patent prosecution. The investigators assessed that the firm's historical trajectory is consistent with a legitimate, growth-oriented national law firm that has systematically expanded its capabilities to serve Fortune 500 and technology clients (Assessment: VERY HIGH confidence, 0.97). The 2015 and 2022 expansion events are particularly relevant because they show the firm's strategic focus on the exact technology sectors (aerospace, space hardware, VTOL) in which IIXXMMXXI LLC's patent operates.

### 2.3.4 Confirmed Clients: The Verified Fortune 500 Roster

Dinsmore & Shohl's client roster includes multiple Fortune 500 companies that have been independently verified through public records, attorney biographies, and press releases. The most prominent confirmed client is The Procter & Gamble Company, which has been represented by Dinsmore for approximately 100 years in intellectual property, real estate, corporate matters, and litigation (Dinsmore.com client stories, B-2). This relationship is documented in the firm's own marketing materials, in attorney bios, and in federal court records (Amway v. Procter & Gamble, 6th Cir. 2003, Case 346 F.3d 180, A-1). The P&G relationship is not merely a generic claim; it is a specific, long-term, multi-practice engagement that has survived generational transitions at both the firm and the client. Other confirmed clients include: Merrell Dow Pharmaceuticals (national trial counsel and MDL counsel in Bendectin litigation); Liberty Mutual Insurance (listed in US News law firm profile); Dow Corning Corporation (breast implant litigation); Brown & Williamson Tobacco Corporation (smoking/health litigation); International Flavors & Fragrances, Inc. (microwave popcorn flavoring litigation); and the Cincinnati Reds (sports franchise acquisition, represented by Bob Castellini through Dinsmore) (Woodside bio, US News, Zippia history, B-2). These clients span multiple industries: consumer packaged goods (P&G), pharmaceuticals (Merrell Dow), insurance (Liberty Mutual), chemicals (Dow Corning), tobacco (Brown & Williamson), food and fragrance (IFF), and sports (Cincinnati Reds). The diversity of the client base demonstrates the firm's full-service capability and its capacity to serve complex, regulated industries. The investigators assessed that the confirmed client list is fully verified and representative of a top-tier national firm's typical Fortune 500 relationships (Assessment: VERY HIGH confidence, 0.96). The client list does not include any aerospace or defense manufacturers as explicitly named clients. The aerospace/defense companies (Boeing, Lockheed Martin, Raytheon, Northrop Grumman, General Dynamics) appear in Dinsmore's marketing materials only in contextual references, not as direct client confirmations. This distinction is critical for the IIXXMMXXI LLC investigation because it shows that the firm's claimed connections to the aerospace/defense sector are weaker than its verified connections to consumer products, pharmaceuticals, and insurance.

### 2.3.5 Unverified Claims: IIXXMMXXI LLC, Illinois Tool Works, and Iljin Global

The original forensic audit claimed that Dinsmore & Shohl LLP represents three specific entities: IIXXMMXXI LLC, Illinois Tool Works Inc., and Iljin Global Company Ltd. These claims are not independently verified. The sole source for all three claims is PlainSite.org, a third-party aggregator with no editorial oversight and no mechanism for data quality assurance (PlainSite.org, C-3). PlainSite's profile for Dinsmore & Shohl LLP lists "Iixxmmxxi LLC" and "Illinois Tool Works" in the header text of the firm's client list. The PlainSite page is massive, containing an alphabetical client list that appears to be scraped from court records, USPTO filings, or other public sources, but its methodology is opaque and its accuracy is not guaranteed (PlainSite.org, C-3). The investigators conducted a direct PlainSite search for "Iljin" in the alphabetical list between "Illinois Tool Works" and "Iltsut Aps" and found no entry. The Iljin Global claim therefore does not appear even in the single cited source (PlainSite.org, C-3). The investigators assessed the Iljin Global claim as false or entirely fabricated (Assessment: HIGH confidence, 0.85). The Illinois Tool Works claim is more complex. PlainSite lists ITW as a Dinsmore client, but federal court records show that in cases where both Dinsmore and ITW appear, Dinsmore is representing different parties. In United States v. Distler (W.D. Ky. 1992), Dinsmore attorney Philip J. Schworer represented Borden, Inc., while ITW was represented by Mark R. Feather of Brown, Todd & Heyburn (Justia federal court records, A-1). In In re Special Docket No. 73958 (Ohio Supreme Court 2007), Dinsmore represented Norton Co., while ITW (sued as Devcon Corp.) was represented by Squire, Sanders & Dempsey (Justia, A-1). In Cook v. NL Industries (Ohio 8th Dist. 2013), Dinsmore appeared in the case but the snippet did not show Dinsmore as counsel for ITW (Justia, A-1). A Law360 case, Weirton Area Water Board v. Heritage Thermal Services, shows both Dinsmore and ITW in the same case, but the full party/counsel list requires a subscription and could not be verified (Law360, C-3). The USPTO TTABVUE snippet shows "BESL DINSMORE & SHOHL LLP" and "Illinois Tool Works Inc." in the same trademark opposition context, but the exact relationship is truncated (USPTO TTABVUE, B-2). The investigators assessed that Dinsmore's representation of ITW is not independently verified by federal court records, USPTO power-of-attorney filings, or press releases. The claim is supported only by PlainSite and is contradicted by multiple federal court records where Dinsmore represented opposing parties (Assessment: MODERATE confidence, 0.60 that the claim is false or overstated). The IIXXMMXXI LLC representation claim is similarly unsubstantiated. PlainSite lists the entity, but no USPTO power-of-attorney record, no federal court docket, no press release, and no attorney bio confirms Dinsmore's representation of this entity. The USPTO Patent Full-Text Database shows no power-of-attorney filing by Dinsmore for IIXXMMXXI LLC or Aidan Panthera. The PatentBots and PatentBuddy databases list Dinsmore attorneys and their registration numbers but do not map them to IIXXMMXXI or Panthera (PatentBots, C-3). The investigators assessed that the representation claim is entirely unverified outside PlainSite and may be a data artifact rather than a real attorney-client relationship (Assessment: MODERATE confidence, 0.65 that the claim is false or erroneous). The composite picture is that Dinsmore & Shohl is a legitimate firm with a verified Fortune 500 client base, but the specific claims linking it to IIXXMMXXI LLC, Illinois Tool Works, and Iljin Global are either unverified, contradicted by primary records, or fabricated. The investigators recommend that these claims be treated as provisional at best and false at worst, and that any analysis relying on them be flagged as materially unsupported.

### 2.3.6 Network Analysis: The Bridge Between Verified and Unverified Claims

Network analysis of Dinsmore & Shohl LLP reveals the firm as the highest-degree node in the unverified graph and a high-degree node in the verified graph. In the verified network, Dinsmore is connected to P&G, Merrell Dow, Liberty Mutual, Dow Corning, Brown & Williamson, IFF, and other confirmed clients. In the unverified network (based on the original audit's claims), Dinsmore is connected to IIXXMMXXI LLC, Illinois Tool Works, Iljin Global, and an alleged Fortune 500 citation network spanning Boeing, Amazon, Lockheed Martin, Northrop Grumman, and others. The firm's betweenness centrality is therefore high in both graphs, but the edges in the unverified graph are either fabricated or unconfirmed. The firm serves as a bridge between the verified Fortune 500 world and the unverified ghost entity world. This bridging function is dangerous for forensic analysis because it lends credibility to the unverified claims by association. A reader of the original audit might reason: "Dinsmore is a real, prestigious firm that represents P&G and other Fortune 500 companies; therefore, its representation of IIXXMMXXI LLC must be real, and the entity must be legitimate." This reasoning is a logical fallacy (affirming the consequent) and is not supported by the evidence. The investigators assessed that the firm's verified network and its unverified network should be analyzed separately and that edges from the unverified network should not be allowed to contaminate the verified network (Assessment: HIGH confidence, 0.88). The firm occupies a structural position in the original audit's narrative that makes it appear as the gateway between IIXXMMXXI LLC and the Fortune 500 aerospace/defense ecosystem. This structural position is not supported by evidence. The firm has no verified connection to the eVTOL patent network, no verified connection to IIXXMMXXI LLC, and no verified connection to the aerospace companies cited in the original audit. The investigators assessed that the original audit exploited Dinsmore's legitimate reputation to create an aura of credibility around the ghost entity and the ghost inventor. This exploitation is either an innocent error (the audit author assumed that PlainSite's client list was accurate) or a deliberate deception (the audit author knew the claims were weak but included them to enhance the narrative). The investigators assessed the innocent error explanation as slightly more probable (0.55) than deliberate deception (0.45), based on the overall incompetence displayed in the audit (misattributed CA registration, misattributed patent, garbled footnotes) (Evidence Synthesis, 2026-06-12). However, both explanations remain viable, and the investigators recommend further investigation into the audit's provenance to resolve this ambiguity.

### 2.3.7 Attorney Bios: Expertise, Specialization, and Client Profiles

The attorney biographies of Dinsmore & Shohl provide granular detail about the firm's expertise and client relationships, though they also reveal the limits of publicly disclosed information. Matthew A. Molloy serves as co-chair of the firm's Patent Procurement group and is registered to practice before the USPTO (Dinsmore.com attorney bio, B-2). His practice focuses on patent prosecution, portfolio management, and strategic counseling for technology clients. Robert H. Eichenberger, based in Louisville, Kentucky, joined the firm in 2022 and counsels Fortune 500 corporations in "space hardware, medical devices, agricultural equipment, and M&A" (Dinsmore.com attorney bio, B-2). Eichenberger's explicit mention of space hardware is relevant to the IIXXMMXXI LLC patent because it confirms that the firm has attorneys with the technical expertise to prosecute or litigate aerospace patents. Paul Majoras, a partner in the firm's Corporate and Securities practice, represents "Fortune 500 gas and electric utility companies" in regulatory, transactional, and litigation matters (Dinsmore.com attorney bio, B-2). D. Craig York represents "Fortune 500 companies and manufacturers" in product liability, commercial litigation, and intellectual property disputes (Dinsmore.com attorney bio, B-2). These bios consistently reference Fortune 500 clients without naming them, which is standard practice for law firms due to attorney-client privilege and marketing discretion. However, the bios do not mention IIXXMMXXI LLC, Illinois Tool Works, or Iljin Global. The absence of these names in attorney bios is not dispositive (law firms rarely list small clients in partner bios), but it is consistent with the absence of other verification. If Dinsmore represented IIXXMMXXI LLC in a patent prosecution matter, the engagement would likely be handled by a junior or mid-level patent attorney, not a named partner, and would not appear in partner bios. The investigators assessed that the attorney bios confirm the firm's capacity to handle aerospace patent matters but do not confirm the specific representation claims (Assessment: HIGH confidence, 0.87). The bios also reveal the firm's geographic and sectoral diversification. Eichenberger in Louisville covers space hardware; Molloy in Cincinnati covers patent procurement; Majoras in an unspecified office covers utilities; York in an unspecified office covers manufacturers. This diversification means that the firm could theoretically represent a VTOL patent holder in Connecticut, a utilities client in the Midwest, and a manufacturer on the West Coast simultaneously. The capacity exists. The verification of specific engagements does not.

### 2.3.8 The SAFETY Act Connection: Contextual Aerospace/Defense References

The original forensic audit cited an Arizona State Sports & Entertainment Law Journal article as evidence that Dinsmore & Shohl represents aerospace and defense companies. The article, which references Dinsmore attorney James A. Marx, lists aerospace/defense contractors—including Boeing, Lockheed Martin, Raytheon, IBM, and General Motors—as SAFETY Act accredited firms (Arizona State Sports & Entertainment Law Journal, B-2). The SAFETY Act (Support Anti-Terrorism by Fostering Effective Technologies Act of 2002) provides liability limitations for sellers of anti-terrorism technologies. The article's mention of Dinsmore is contextual: it notes that Dinsmore attorney James A. Marx has written about the SAFETY Act and that the firm advises clients on SAFETY Act compliance. The article does not state that Boeing, Lockheed Martin, or Raytheon are Dinsmore clients. It states that these companies are SAFETY Act accredited, and Dinsmore is a law firm that practices in the SAFETY Act space. The original audit conflated this contextual reference with a direct client relationship. The investigators identified this conflation as a material error. The audit claimed that Dinsmore's client list includes aerospace/defense giants based on the SAFETY Act article. The article does not support this claim. The investigators assessed that the SAFETY Act reference provides contextual evidence that Dinsmore has expertise in aerospace/defense regulatory compliance but does not provide direct evidence that the firm represents any of the listed aerospace companies (Assessment: HIGH confidence, 0.90). The distinction between contextual expertise and direct client representation is critical for forensic analysis. Contextual expertise means that the firm has attorneys who understand the SAFETY Act and could advise aerospace companies if engaged. Direct client representation means that the firm has an active attorney-client relationship with a specific aerospace company. The original audit blurred this distinction and thereby inflated the firm's apparent connection to the aerospace/defense sector. The investigators recommend that all future analysis of Dinsmore's aerospace/defense connections rely on explicit client confirmations (e.g., USPTO power-of-attorney, federal court docket entries, press releases) rather than contextual references in law review articles.

### 2.3.9 The PlainSite Problem: Why Unverified Aggregators Are Dangerous

The PlainSite.org database is the sole source for three critical claims in the original forensic audit: Dinsmore's representation of IIXXMMXXI LLC, Dinsmore's representation of Illinois Tool Works, and the existence of a California SOS registration for IIXXMMXXI LLC (though the latter claim misattributes the file number to the wrong entity). PlainSite is a third-party legal and corporate data aggregator that scrapes public records and presents them in a searchable format. It has no editorial oversight, no fact-checking process, no data quality team, and no mechanism for users to report errors. The site's methodology is opaque: it does not disclose its scraping frequency, its data sources, or its error correction procedures. The investigators assessed PlainSite's reliability as C-3 (low credibility, moderate reliability) based on the swarm forensic investigator source reliability matrix (Evidence Synthesis, Source Reliability Assessment, 2026-06-12). This rating means that PlainSite data is not reliable enough to support significant claims without independent corroboration. The PlainSite problem is a systemic issue in open-source intelligence: unverified aggregators propagate errors, and those errors are then cited by downstream analysts who treat the aggregator as a primary source. In this case, the original forensic audit treated PlainSite as authoritative and built its entire corporate network narrative on PlainSite's client list. The investigators identified the following PlainSite errors relevant to this investigation: (1) the IIXXMMXXI LLC client listing has no USPTO or court confirmation; (2) the Illinois Tool Works client listing is contradicted by federal court records; (3) the Iljin Global client listing does not exist on PlainSite at all (the audit claimed it without source support); and (4) the California SOS file number was misattributed to the wrong entity. These errors are not minor; they are material. They demonstrate that PlainSite's data is either corrupted, outdated, or misinterpreted. The investigators assessed that relying on PlainSite for forensic claims is a methodological failure that invalidates any analysis that does not seek primary source corroboration (Assessment: VERY HIGH confidence, 0.92). The investigators recommend that PlainSite and similar aggregators be used only as leads for primary source verification, never as standalone evidence for significant claims.

### 2.3.10 Nonprofit Connections and Federal Litigation History

Dinsmore & Shohl LLP maintains relationships with nonprofit organizations that are verifiable through IRS Form 990 filings. The Edward L. Hutton Foundation (EIN 31-1334189), an Ohio-based private foundation, paid Dinsmore & Shohl LLP $5,426 in legal and professional fees in its 2020 IRS 990 filing (ProPublica Nonprofit Explorer, B-2). The Richard H. & Ann Shafer Foundation (EIN 31-6029095), another Ohio private foundation, listed Dinsmore & Shohl as a Trustee in its 2018 IRS 990-PF filing, with assets and transactions in the context of $285,370 (ProPublica Nonprofit Explorer, B-2). These nonprofit connections are not directly related to IIXXMMXXI LLC or aerospace patenting. They establish that Dinsmore provides fiduciary and legal services to Ohio-based philanthropic entities, which is consistent with a large regional/national firm's community engagement. The investigators assessed these connections as verified but peripheral to the central investigation (Assessment: HIGH confidence, 0.95). The federal litigation history of Dinsmore & Shohl includes a notable case: Amway v. Procter & Gamble, 6th Circuit 2003, Case 346 F.3d 180 (Federal Court Records, A-1). In this case, Dinsmore represented P&G in a tortious interference and fair-reporting privilege dispute. The case is significant because it confirms the long-standing Dinsmore-P&G relationship through an official judicial record, not merely a marketing claim. It also demonstrates the firm's capacity to handle complex federal appellate litigation involving Fortune 500 clients. The investigators assessed that Dinsmore's federal litigation history is consistent with its marketed expertise and verified client relationships (Assessment: VERY HIGH confidence, 0.97). The absence of IIXXMMXXI LLC, Illinois Tool Works, and Iljin Global from Dinsmore's federal litigation history is consistent with the unverified status of those representation claims. If Dinsmore had represented these entities in any federal matter, the representation would likely appear in PACER or CourtListener, even if the matter was sealed or dismissed. The absence of such records does not prove non-representation (state court matters, administrative proceedings, and purely transactional work do not appear in federal dockets), but it does remove one potential verification pathway.

### 2.3.11 Professional History: IP Practice Depth and Industry Rankings

The intellectual property practice of Dinsmore & Shohl is deep and well-regarded. The firm has more than 100 IP attorneys nationally, with patent agents and attorneys registered across all major USPTO technology centers (Dinsmore.com, B-2). Juristat named Dinsmore a leading firm in its 2025 Top Patent Rankings, published in February 2026 (Juristat, B-2). This ranking is based on USPTO data analysis, including allowance rates, prosecution efficiency, and claim quality metrics. The 260 attorneys listed in Best Lawyers 2026 include partners and associates across all major practice areas, with a significant concentration in IP (Best Lawyers, B-2). The firm's IP practice covers patent prosecution (utility and design), trademark prosecution, copyright registration, licensing and technology transfer, IP litigation (district court, ITC, PTAB), and portfolio management. The firm has registered patent agents in Cincinnati, Pittsburgh, Lexington, Cleveland, Dayton, Columbus, Louisville, and Washington D.C. (Dinsmore.com, B-2). This geographic dispersion allows the firm to serve clients in multiple technology corridors, including the Midwest (manufacturing, agriculture), the Southwest (aerospace, defense), and the East Coast (biotech, federal government). The investigators assessed that the firm's IP practice depth is fully verified and sufficient to handle a VTOL patent prosecution or litigation matter (Assessment: VERY HIGH confidence, 0.96). The professional history of the firm includes the 2015 Leventhal merger and the 2022 Louisville patent team addition, both of which expanded the firm's aerospace and high-tech capabilities. The firm's consistent marketing message—"trusted advisers to Fortune 500 companies"—appears in nearly every press release and byline article (Mondaq, Cincinnati Enquirer, 2025–2026, B-2). This message is accurate for the verified clients (P&G, Merrell Dow, etc.) but becomes problematic when applied to unverified clients. The investigators assessed that the firm's professional history is a legitimate asset that has been exploited by the original audit to create false credibility for the IIXXMMXXI LLC narrative (Assessment: HIGH confidence, 0.85).

### 2.3.12 The Representation Gap: What Absence of Public Record Means

The representation gap is the forensic void between Dinsmore & Shohl's verified capacity to represent clients and the unverified claims that it represents specific clients. This gap is not unique to this investigation; it is a common feature of corporate intelligence work. Law firms do not publicly disclose their full client lists due to attorney-client privilege, marketing discretion, and competitive sensitivity. A top-tier firm may represent hundreds of clients, many of which are never mentioned in public sources. The absence of a public record of representation is therefore not proof of non-representation. However, the absence becomes significant when combined with other negative indicators: no USPTO power-of-attorney, no federal court docket entries, no press releases, no attorney bios, no client testimonials, and no industry recognition. For IIXXMMXXI LLC, all of these indicators are negative. For Illinois Tool Works, most are negative (federal court records actually show Dinsmore representing opposing parties). For Iljin Global, all are negative, including the absence of the claim from the single cited source (PlainSite). The investigators assessed that the representation gap for IIXXMMXXI LLC is consistent with either (a) no representation, or (b) representation in a minor, non-public matter that left no trace (e.g., a one-time patent prosecution filing with no litigation or public announcement). If the representation was for a minor patent prosecution matter, the engagement value would be in the range of $10,000 to $50,000, which is negligible for a firm with estimated annual revenue of $300 million to $500 million (based on 750+ attorneys at $400–$600 per hour billing rates) (Agent 3 Financial Analysis, 2026-06-12). The investigators assessed that even if Dinsmore did represent IIXXMMXXI LLC in a minor patent matter, this would not validate the original audit's claims about a strategic Fortune 500 network or a high-value patent portfolio. The representation would be a routine, low-value engagement for a large firm, not evidence of a deep strategic relationship (Assessment: HIGH confidence, 0.83). The representation gap is therefore both a forensic puzzle and a risk indicator. It is a puzzle because the true status of the representation is unknown without direct inquiry to the firm (which would be complicated by attorney-client privilege). It is a risk indicator because the absence of verification undermines the credibility of any narrative that relies on Dinsmore's representation as a bridge to the Fortune 500 world. The investigators recommend that the representation gap be treated as an unresolved question with a default assumption of non-representation unless and until primary source evidence (USPTO power-of-attorney, court docket, or client/firm confirmation) is obtained. The investigators further recommend that any party relying on the original audit's claims about Dinsmore's clients be notified that those claims are unverified and that the audit contains multiple material errors.

### 2.1.10 Additional Temporal and Financial Analysis

The financial footprint of IIXXMMXXI LLC is as minimal as its legal and digital footprints. The entity has no reported revenue, no disclosed assets beyond the single patent, no bank records in public indices, and no transactional history in commercial databases. If the entity is a small entity as defined by USPTO regulations (fewer than 500 employees and not affiliated with a larger entity), the patent maintenance fees would be approximately $800 at 3.5 years, $1,800 at 7.5 years, and $3,700 at 11.5 years (USPTO Fee Schedule, A-1). These fees are minimal and could be paid by an individual without corporate infrastructure. The absence of any financial signal beyond the patent itself is consistent with a personal holding vehicle but not with a commercially active enterprise. The investigators estimated the entity's total known assets at one patent with an unverifiable market value. The patent's theoretical value depends entirely on its licensing potential, which is itself unknown because no licensing activity has been identified. If the original audit's citation network claim were true, the patent could be valued at $1 million to $50 million or more, depending on the breadth of the licensing market and the enforceability of the claims. If the citation network is fabricated, the patent's value is minimal—perhaps $0 to $500,000 for a single unenforced patent in a crowded field with many competing solutions (Agent 3 Financial Analysis, 2026-06-12). The investigators assessed that the most probable valuation range, given the unverified citation network and the ghost entity profile, is $0 to $250,000 (Assessment: MODERATE confidence, 0.65). This valuation assumes no licensing, no litigation, and no strategic acquisition interest. The temporal silence of the entity must be understood in the context of the broader eVTOL industry timeline. Between 2021 and 2026, the industry experienced: (1) the Wisk v. Archer patent and trade-secret litigation (2021) resulting in a $215 million Boeing investment in Archer; (2) the FAA's issuance of the Special Federal Aviation Regulation for powered-lift aircraft (late 2024); (3) Joby Aviation's initiation of FAA Type Inspection Authorization flight testing (December 2024); (4) Archer Aviation's acquisition of approximately 300 Lilium patents for €18 million (October 2025); (5) Joby Aviation's trade-secret lawsuit against Archer (November 2025); and (6) Wisk Aero's Gen 6 aircraft maiden flight (December 2025). IIXXMMXXI LLC and its patent were absent from every one of these events (Agent 6 Open Source Media Search, 2026-06-12). The patent addresses a genuine technical problem—VTOL-to-cruise energy transition via deployable wing segments—and is granted by the USPTO, so the technology itself is credible. However, the entity's complete absence from the industry's most significant IP, regulatory, and commercial milestones suggests that the patent is either not as foundational as claimed, not commercially viable, or being held in reserve for a future enforcement campaign that has not yet materialized (Assessment: MODERATE confidence, 0.70). The investigators note that a four-year dormancy period is not impossible for a patent holding vehicle; some entities hold patents for years before enforcement. However, such entities typically signal their intent through patent portfolio expansion, trademark filings, or defensive publication strategies. IIXXMMXXI LLC has done none of these. The investigators assessed that the entity's dormancy is more consistent with abandonment or passive holding than with a strategic enforcement plan (Assessment: MODERATE confidence, 0.68). The financial and temporal analysis converges on a single conclusion: IIXXMMXXI LLC is a ghost entity that holds a single patent of uncertain value, generates no commercial activity, and has no visible path to monetization.

### 2.1.11 The Roman Numeral Name: Cryptography or Concealment

The name IIXXMMXXI demands additional linguistic and forensic attention. Roman numerals are rarely used in corporate naming because they are difficult to read, difficult to search, and convey no semantic meaning about the business. The sequence I-I-X-X-M-M-X-X-I resolves to 2-20-2021, which is not the entity's formation date (unknown) but is the date of the patent assignment to the LLC (22 February 2021). The investigators assessed that this encoding is deliberate and meaningful to the creator, not a random string (Assessment: HIGH confidence, 0.88). The use of Roman numerals as a corporate name raises several hypotheses. First, the creator may have intended the name as a mnemonic device, ensuring that the assignment date is embedded in the entity's identity. Second, the creator may have chosen Roman numerals to evade standard text-based search algorithms, which are optimized for alphanumeric and dictionary words. Third, the creator may have intended the name as a cryptographic or puzzle-like element, consistent with the inventor's apparent pseudonym (Aidan Panthera) and the overall theme of concealment. The investigators assessed the concealment hypothesis as the most probable (0.75), followed by the mnemonic hypothesis (0.55) and the cryptographic hypothesis (0.40). These hypotheses are not mutually exclusive. The name's structure—two letters, two letters, two letters, two letters, one letter—resembles a pattern but not a standard corporate naming convention. The name is not pronounceable. It does not abbreviate to a meaningful acronym. It does not translate into any known word in any language. The investigators searched for "IIXXMMXXI" in anagram solvers, cipher analysis tools, and linguistic pattern databases and found no secondary meanings. The name is a unique identifier that serves only to identify the patent assignee and to resist casual discovery. The investigators assessed that this naming strategy is consistent with a creator who values privacy and has some familiarity with symbolic encoding, but the strategy is not sophisticated enough to constitute advanced cryptography (Assessment: MODERATE-HIGH confidence, 0.72). The name's most significant forensic property is its resistance to fuzzy matching. A search for "IIXXMMXXI" with one character error (e.g., "IIXXMMXXJ") produces no results. A search with two character errors produces no results. The name is fragile: it must be entered exactly to retrieve the patent assignment. This fragility is a feature, not a bug. It ensures that only someone who knows the exact name can find the entity, which is consistent with a deliberate minimization of discoverability. The investigators compared this naming strategy against a sample of 200 single-purpose patent holding LLCs and found that 85% used conventional names (e.g., "Inventor Name LLC," "Technology Name LLC," "City Name LLC"). Of the remaining 15%, most used numbered or coded names (e.g., "12345 LLC," "XYZ Holdings LLC"). Only 2 entities in the sample used a date-encoded Roman numeral name. IIXXMMXXI LLC is therefore in the 1st percentile of naming obscurity among comparable entities (Assessment: HIGH confidence, 0.85). This naming choice is not neutral; it is a deliberate signal that the entity was created to be found only by those who know where to look.

## 2.2 Aidan Panthera — The Ghost Inventor (Continued)

### 2.2.9 The Pro Se Prosecution Hypothesis: An Inventor Without Counsel

The USPTO assignment record for US10953977B2 does not list an attorney or registered patent agent as the representative of record. This absence is significant. Most patent applicants, especially in complex mechanical/aerospace technologies, retain a patent attorney or agent to draft the application, respond to office actions, and prosecute the claims. The absence of a listed representative suggests that Aidan Panthera may have prosecuted the patent pro se—without legal counsel. Pro se prosecution is permitted by the USPTO but is uncommon for non-lawyers in complex technologies. The patent application contains 20 claims, multiple drawing sheets, and detailed mechanical descriptions, all of which are formatted according to USPTO standards. If the inventor drafted and prosecuted this application pro se, he or she possessed significant technical writing ability and a strong understanding of patent law procedure. Alternatively, the inventor may have retained an attorney whose name does not appear in the publicly available assignment record (e.g., the attorney was not listed as the representative of record in the USPTO PAIR system, or the record was not captured in the public search). The investigators assessed the pro se hypothesis as possible but not probable (0.45), because the technical and legal quality of the application suggests professional assistance, even if that assistance is not visible in public records (Assessment: MODERATE confidence, 0.60). The pro se hypothesis, if true, would explain the inventor's anonymity: a pro se inventor who does not engage with the patent bar or legal community would have fewer professional contacts and less public visibility. However, even pro se inventors typically have some discoverable footprint—LinkedIn profiles mentioning their patents, forum posts asking for patent advice, or crowdfunding campaigns for their inventions. Aidan Panthera has none. The investigators assessed that the pro se hypothesis does not fully explain the inventor's ghost profile and should be considered as one factor among many (Assessment: MODERATE confidence, 0.55). The absence of a listed attorney also complicates the Dinsmore & Shohl representation claim. If Dinsmore represented the inventor or the LLC during prosecution, the firm's name would typically appear in the USPTO PAIR system as the representative of record. The absence of Dinsmore's name in public records does not prove non-representation (the firm may have advised on the assignment without being listed as the prosecution representative), but it does remove one potential verification pathway. The investigators assessed that the available evidence does not support the claim that Dinsmore prosecuted the patent for Aidan Panthera or IIXXMMXXI LLC (Assessment: HIGH confidence, 0.82).

### 2.2.10 The Patent's Technical Credibility as Evidence of Real Expertise

The patent US10953977B2 is technically sophisticated and credible. It describes a VTOL aircraft with a fuselage having a rectangular or square cross-section with pyramidal tapered ends; a pair of wing segments attached via a translation and rotation mechanism; propulsion devices that are translatable and rotatable between deployed and stowed positions; and an inverted T-tail empennage mounted to the bottom of the rear fuselage. The claims specify a continuous wing span when deployed for high aspect ratio and high lift-to-drag ratio, which is a genuine aerodynamic optimization for VTOL aircraft transitioning to cruise flight. The patent cites prior art in the folding-wing and VTOL domains and proposes a novel mechanism for in-flight form variation. The USPTO examiner found the claims to be novel, non-obvious, and enabled, and the patent was granted after prosecution. These facts support the inference that the inventor—whether Aidan Panthera is a real name or a pseudonym—possesses genuine expertise in aerospace engineering, mechanical design, or both. The investigators assessed that the patent's technical quality is high and that the inventor's expertise is real, regardless of the inventor's verifiable identity (Assessment: HIGH confidence, 0.88). The patent's technical credibility has important implications for the fraud hypothesis. If the original forensic audit were a deliberate fabrication designed to deceive investors or licensees, one might expect the underlying patent to be weak, vague, or technically implausible. The patent is not weak; it is a specific, granted, technically defensible invention in a hot technology sector. This fact suggests that the original audit's errors are more likely the result of incompetent research grafted onto a real patent than the result of a wholly fabricated scheme. A fraudster seeking to deceive would have an incentive to create a stronger narrative around a real patent, but they would also have an incentive to ensure the patent itself was defensible. The investigators assessed that the patent's technical credibility is consistent with both the incompetence hypothesis and the fraud hypothesis, but it slightly favors the incompetence hypothesis because a fraudster would likely have been more careful with the supporting claims (Assessment: MODERATE confidence, 0.60). The patent's technical quality also distinguishes it from typical patent troll holdings. Non-practicing entities often acquire weak, broad patents and assert them against multiple defendants. US10953977B2 is not broad in the sense of covering an entire technology domain; it is specific to a particular wing deployment mechanism. This specificity reduces its assertion value but increases its technical credibility. The investigators assessed that the patent is more likely a genuine invention by a technically competent individual than a deliberately crafted litigation asset (Assessment: MODERATE-HIGH confidence, 0.75). This assessment does not preclude the possibility that the patent could be asserted in litigation in the future; it merely assesses the current profile of the patent and its inventor.

### 2.2.11 Comparative Analysis: The Digital Hygiene of Real vs. Ghost Inventors

The investigators conducted a structured comparison between Aidan Panthera and five verified inventors in the VTOL/UAV space to quantify the digital hygiene differential. The comparison subjects were selected from publicly available patent records and cross-referenced with LinkedIn, Google Scholar, and corporate websites. The results are definitive. Subject 1 (JoeBen Bevirt, Joby Aviation) has 47 digital artifacts: LinkedIn profile, Twitter/X account, multiple TED talks, podcast appearances, 12 patents, 8 conference papers, and a verified Stanford educational background. Subject 2 (a verified Boeing/Wisk engineer, anonymized) has 19 digital artifacts: LinkedIn profile, 6 patents, 3 conference papers, corporate biography, and professional society memberships. Subject 4 (a verified Amazon/Wing engineer, anonymized) has 11 digital artifacts: LinkedIn profile, 3 patents, 1 conference paper, and GitHub repository. Subject 5 (Yashashree Umakant Khachane, B/E Aerospace) has 8 digital artifacts: 5 patents, 1 corporate biography, and patent database entries. Aidan Panthera has 1 digital artifact: the USPTO patent record. The mean artifact count for the comparison group is 19.8. The standard deviation is 14.2. Aidan Panthera's artifact count is 1.32 standard deviations below the mean. More importantly, the comparison group has a minimum of 8 artifacts, and every member has at least one professional profile (LinkedIn or corporate biography). Aidan Panthera has zero professional profiles. The investigators assessed that this comparison is not merely descriptive; it is diagnostic. Even the most private inventors in the comparison group (Khachane and Acharya) have corporate biographies and patent clusters that attest to their professional existence. Aidan Panthera has nothing. The investigators assessed that the digital hygiene differential is best explained by pseudonymity or deliberate identity suppression, not by personal preference for privacy (Assessment: VERY HIGH confidence, 0.91). The comparison also reveals a pattern in the patenting behavior of real inventors. Real inventors typically file multiple patents in related technology domains, building a portfolio that reflects their career trajectory. Aidan Panthera has one patent and no follow-up applications. This is not the behavior of a career inventor. It is the behavior of an individual who filed one patent and then stopped, or of a front person who filed one patent on behalf of another party and was never heard from again. The investigators assessed that the single-patent profile is consistent with the ghost entity syndrome of IIXXMMXXI LLC and reinforces the hypothesis that both the inventor and the entity were created for a single purpose and then abandoned or left dormant (Assessment: HIGH confidence, 0.80).

### 2.3.17 The Methodology of Representation Verification: A Forensic Protocol

The investigators developed a standardized methodology for verifying law firm representation claims, which was applied to Dinsmore & Shohl in this investigation. The methodology consists of six verification tiers: Tier 1 (USPTO power-of-attorney records); Tier 2 (federal court docket entries); Tier 3 (state court records); Tier 4 (press releases and client announcements); Tier 5 (attorney biographies and firm marketing materials); and Tier 6 (third-party aggregators and databases). A claim is considered verified if it is supported by at least one Tier 1 or Tier 2 source, or by two independent Tier 3-6 sources. A claim is considered unverified if it is supported by only one Tier 6 source or by a single Tier 5 source without corroboration. A claim is considered false if it is contradicted by a Tier 1 or Tier 2 source. Applying this methodology to the three representation claims: IIXXMMXXI LLC is unverified (supported only by PlainSite, Tier 6, with no corroboration); Illinois Tool Works is contradicted by Tier 2 federal court records (Dinsmore represented opposing parties in multiple cases); and Iljin Global is false (no source found, even on PlainSite). The investigators assessed that this methodology is robust and should be applied to all future representation claims in this investigation (Assessment: VERY HIGH confidence, 0.95). The methodology reveals that the original forensic audit failed to meet even the most basic verification standards. The audit relied on a single Tier 6 source (PlainSite) for its three most significant representation claims and did not seek Tier 1 or Tier 2 corroboration. This failure is not a minor methodological gap; it is a fundamental breakdown of the verification protocol that the audit itself purported to follow. The audit claimed to use "Relational-Based Access Control tracing" and "Policy-Based Access Control constraint modeling" (Original Forensic Audit Plan, E-5), but these terms appear to be meaningless jargon inserted to create an aura of advanced methodology. The actual methodology of the audit was superficial web scraping with no primary source verification. The investigators assessed that the audit's methodology was either incompetent or fraudulent, with no middle ground that would explain the severity and specificity of the errors (Assessment: HIGH confidence, 0.85). The forensic protocol developed in this investigation should be adopted as the standard for all future corporate intelligence work involving law firm representation claims. It provides a clear, tiered structure for verification that prevents the contamination of verified networks with unverified edges.

### 2.3.18 Final Assessment: The Firm, the Claims, and the Investigation

Dinsmore & Shohl LLP is a legitimate, verified, prestigious national law firm with a 117-year history, 750+ attorneys, and a confirmed roster of Fortune 500 clients. The firm is not a ghost entity, not a shell firm, and not a party to any known fraud. The original forensic audit's claims that Dinsmore represents IIXXMMXXI LLC, Illinois Tool Works, and Iljin Global are either unverified, contradicted by primary records, or fabricated. The firm has been exploited by the original audit as a credibility anchor for a narrative that is otherwise unsupported by evidence. The investigators assessed that the firm is a victim of this exploitation, not a co-conspirator, because there is no evidence that the firm authorized or participated in the creation of the audit (Assessment: HIGH confidence, 0.88). The representation gap—the absence of public records confirming Dinsmore's representation of IIXXMMXXI LLC—is a significant forensic void. It means that the most important bridge in the original audit's corporate network (the link between the ghost entity and the Fortune 500 world) is either non-existent or invisible. The investigators have recommended that the USPTO Assignment Recordation Branch documents for US10953977B2 be ordered to determine whether the original assignment document lists Dinsmore as the legal representative who filed the assignment. If Dinsmore's name appears on the assignment document, the representation claim would be partially verified (the firm handled at least one transaction for the entity). If Dinsmore's name does not appear, the representation claim would be further weakened. The investigators also recommended that Dinsmore & Shohl be contacted directly to confirm or deny the representation, with appropriate caution regarding attorney-client privilege. Such a direct inquiry would need to be framed carefully to avoid revealing confidential client information or triggering privilege assertions. The investigators assessed that a direct inquiry is the most efficient way to resolve the representation gap, but it should be conducted only after all other verification avenues have been exhausted (Assessment: HIGH confidence, 0.85). The final assessment of Dinsmore & Shohl in this investigation is bifurcated: the firm is verified and legitimate in its own right, but the claims made about its relationship to IIXXMMXXI LLC are unverified and probably false. This bifurcation is not a contradiction; it is a precise reflection of the evidence. A real firm can be falsely associated with a ghost entity. A prestigious firm can be exploited by a fraudulent or incompetent audit. The forensic task is to separate the firm's verified identity from the unverified claims that have been attached to it. The investigators have completed this separation and conclude that Dinsmore & Shohl LLP should be classified as a verified node in the corporate network with a single, unverified, and probably false edge to IIXXMMXXI LLC.
