## SECTION 9: LEGAL & REGULATORY ANALYSIS

### 9.1 Applicable Legal Framework

The findings of this investigation implicate multiple legal frameworks across criminal, civil, regulatory, and international domains. The analysis distinguishes between legal standards applicable to the entity IIXXMMXXI LLC itself, standards applicable to the producer of the original forensic audit plan, and standards applicable to any downstream parties who may have relied on or distributed the audit. The legal assessment is conditioned on the factual findings established in Section 10 and does not assume criminal intent where the evidence supports only incompetence or negligence.

**Criminal Statutes Potentially Violated.** The investigation identified four federal criminal statutes that could apply if the original forensic audit plan was used to deceive investors, government officials, or commercial counterparties. First, 18 U.S.C. § 1341 (Mail Fraud) prohibits the use of the U.S. Postal Service or any interstate carrier to execute a scheme to defraud. If the audit document was mailed to potential investors, lenders, or acquisition targets as part of a solicitation, and if the false claims (California registration, second patent, Fortune 500 citation network) were material to the recipient's decision, the elements of mail fraud would be satisfied. The investigation did not locate a mailed copy of the audit, so this application remains hypothetical. The source reliability for the existence of the audit document itself is low (E-5), but the content is sufficiently documented to support legal analysis. Second, 18 U.S.C. § 1343 (Wire Fraud) prohibits the use of electronic communications in furtherance of a fraudulent scheme. If the audit was transmitted by email, uploaded to a cloud platform, or shared through a messaging application to induce reliance, the wire fraud statute would apply. The wire fraud provision has been interpreted broadly by federal courts and requires only a single interstate electronic transmission. The original audit's digital provenance is unknown, so this statute is flagged as a potential basis for prosecution if the transmission channel is established. Third, 18 U.S.C. § 1001 (False Statements) criminalizes knowingly and willfully making materially false statements in any matter within the jurisdiction of the federal government. If the audit was submitted to the USPTO, SEC, DOJ, or any other federal agency, or if it was used in any official proceeding (including civil litigation or arbitration), this statute would apply. The false claims about the California registration and the second patent are material because they directly affect the perceived value and legal standing of the patent asset. Fourth, 35 U.S.C. § 292 (False Marking) prohibits false patent marking, including the misrepresentation of patent ownership or status. While the patent itself is real and properly marked, the audit's claims about a second patent and a corporate registration could constitute false marking if they were used to mislead the public or competitors about the scope of the patent portfolio. The elements of false marking under § 292 require a showing that the patent was falsely marked or that a false statement was made with intent to deceive the public. The audit's claim of a second patent (US11702211) that does not belong to IIXXMMXXI LLC could satisfy this element if the claim was made in a commercial context, such as product labeling, marketing materials, or licensing negotiations. The qui tam provision of § 292 allows any person to sue on behalf of the United States, which creates a broad enforcement mechanism independent of government action. The investigation notes that the False Marking Act was amended in 2011 to require a showing of intent to deceive, raising the evidentiary standard, but the audit's specific and repeated false claims provide a plausible basis for such a showing. The source reliability for the audit's commercial use is low (E-5), so this application remains contingent on establishing that the false claims were communicated to the public or to commercial counterparties.

The criminal statutes identified above are not mutually exclusive. Federal prosecutors could charge multiple counts under a single indictment, and the sentences could run consecutively if the court finds that the offenses caused distinct harms to distinct victims. The investigation emphasizes that the criminal analysis is prospective: no criminal charges have been filed, no grand jury has been empaneled, and no defendant has been identified. The analysis serves to flag the legal risks associated with the audit's content and to guide future investigative and enforcement priorities. The statute of limitations for mail fraud, wire fraud, and false statements is five years from the date of the offense, which provides a substantial window for prosecution if the audit's origin and use are established. The statute of limitations for false marking is also five years. The investigation recommends that any evidence of the audit's distribution be preserved to ensure that the limitations period does not expire before enforcement action can be taken.

**Civil Liability Exposure.** Even in the absence of criminal intent, the producer of the original audit may face civil liability under three theories. First, professional negligence applies if the audit was produced by a licensed professional (attorney, patent agent, investigator, or financial analyst) who breached the applicable duty of care. The duty of care in forensic investigation requires verification of primary sources before asserting factual claims. The audit's failure to verify the California registration against the Secretary of State's official database, its failure to verify the second patent against USPTO records, and its presentation of unverified claims as established facts constitute a clear breach of this duty. If any party relied on the audit to their financial detriment, the elements of professional negligence would be satisfied. Second, fraudulent misrepresentation applies if the false claims were made knowingly with intent to induce reliance and cause financial harm. The audit's specific false claims (exact file number, exact patent number, detailed citation network) suggest a level of precision that is difficult to attribute to mere negligence. The garbled footnotes (Unicode replacement characters), the meaningless technical jargon ("Relational-Based Access Control tracing," "Policy-Based Access Control constraint modeling," "LLM04 heuristics," "ASI01 protocols"), and the misattribution of a real California registration to the wrong entity are indicators that the document may have been constructed to create false authority rather than to report genuine findings. Third, unfair competition applies if the audit was used to inflate the patent's value in merger, acquisition, or licensing negotiations. In the eVTOL sector, where patent valuations drive billion-dollar investment decisions, a fraudulent audit that misrepresents a patent's corporate backing, legal representation, and citation network could distort competitive negotiations and cause actionable harm to competitors or investors. The calculation of damages under fraudulent misrepresentation would include the difference between the price paid and the true value of the patent, plus consequential damages for any lost opportunities or adverse business decisions caused by the reliance. If the audit was used in a securities transaction, the damages could be calculated under the Securities Act or Exchange Act, which provide for rescission, disgorgement, and civil penalties. The professional negligence damages would be limited to the foreseeable harm caused by the breach of duty, which could include the cost of the audit, the cost of corrective investigation, and any reliance damages. The investigation notes that no victim has been identified, and no financial harm has been quantified, so the damages analysis is speculative. However, the potential exposure is substantial given the eVTOL sector's valuation multiples and the audit's material claims.

**Regulatory Violations.** The investigation identified two regulatory regimes that may be implicated. First, SEC Rule 10b-5 prohibits the use of any manipulative or deceptive device in connection with the purchase or sale of securities. If the audit was used in connection with the purchase or sale of securities — for example, to support a SPAC merger, a private placement, or a public offering by Archer, Joby, or any other eVTOL company — the SEC would have jurisdiction. The audit's claims about Fortune 500 citation networks and Dinsmore representation are precisely the type of material information that would affect an investor's valuation of a patent portfolio. The SEC's enforcement pattern in recent years has emphasized "material misrepresentations" about technology assets, including patent portfolios, in SPAC mergers and de-SPAC transactions. The eVTOL sector has seen multiple SPAC mergers (Archer, Joby, Lilium, Vertical Aerospace), and the SEC has brought enforcement actions against companies that misrepresented their IP positions or regulatory approvals. If the audit was used in connection with any of these transactions, the SEC would have a strong basis for investigation. The investigation emphasizes that the SEC's jurisdiction is limited to securities transactions; if the audit was used only in private licensing negotiations, the SEC would not have authority. Second, the Federal Trade Commission Act § 5 prohibits unfair or deceptive acts or practices in commerce. If the audit was used in commercial advertising, investment solicitation, or trade negotiations, the FTC could bring an enforcement action. The FTC's authority extends to business-to-business transactions, not merely consumer advertising, so the use of the audit in patent licensing negotiations would fall within its jurisdiction. The FTC has brought enforcement actions against patent assertion entities that misrepresented their patent portfolios or their licensing rights to deceive competitors. The investigation notes that the FTC's remedial powers are primarily civil and administrative, including injunctions, civil penalties, and consent decrees, but they do not include criminal liability.

**International Law.** If subsequent investigation reveals that IIXXMMXXI LLC is beneficially owned or controlled by a foreign entity, the Committee on Foreign Investment in the United States (CFIUS) regulations under 50 U.S.C. § 4565 may apply. The patent covers VTOL technology with clear dual-use applications (civilian eVTOL and military unmanned aerial systems). Undisclosed foreign control of U.S. critical technology patents triggers mandatory CFIUS filing requirements and can result in forced divestment, civil penalties, and criminal liability. The investigation found no evidence of foreign ownership, but the absence of state registration and beneficial ownership records prevents definitive exclusion. If the entity is registered in an offshore jurisdiction (e.g., British Virgin Islands, Cayman Islands, Marshall Islands), additional international anti-money laundering and corporate transparency frameworks (FATF Recommendations, EU Anti-Tax Avoidance Directive) may apply. The investigation notes that mutual legal assistance may be required to obtain corporate records from offshore jurisdictions if foreign ownership is suspected. The FATF Recommendations, particularly Recommendation 24 (Transparency and Beneficial Ownership of Legal Persons), require jurisdictions to ensure adequate transparency of legal persons, including the maintenance of beneficial ownership information. If IIXXMMXXI LLC is registered in a FATF member jurisdiction, the beneficial ownership information should be available to competent authorities, though not necessarily to the public. The EU's Fifth Anti-Money Laundering Directive (5AMLD) requires member states to maintain central beneficial ownership registers for legal entities, but the registers vary in accessibility and completeness. The investigation's search of EU registers produced no match, but the search was limited to publicly available databases and may not have covered all member states or all registry types. If the entity is registered in a non-FATF, non-EU offshore jurisdiction (e.g., Seychelles, Panama, Nevis), the beneficial ownership information may be held by a registered agent or corporate service provider, and access would require a subpoena or MLAT request. The Patent Cooperation Treaty (PCT) does not directly address beneficial ownership, but it does require applicants to identify their nationality and residence, which could provide clues if the original PCT application for US10953977B2 is examined. The investigation did not locate a PCT record for this patent, suggesting it was filed as a U.S. national application only, which is consistent with a U.S.-based inventor but does not exclude foreign ownership of the LLC.tity is registered in a non-FATF, non-EU offshore jurisdiction (e.g., Seychelles, Panama, Nevis), the beneficial ownership information may be held by a registered agent or corporate service provider, and access would require a subpoena or MLAT request. The Patent Cooperation Treaty (PCT) does not directly address beneficial ownership, but it does require applicants to identify their nationality and residence, which could provide clues if the original PCT application for US10953977B2 is examined. The investigation did not locate a PCT record for this patent, suggesting it was filed as a U.S. national application only, which is consistent with a U.S.-based inventor but does not exclude foreign ownership of the LLC.

### 9.2 Human Rights Assessment

The investigation applied a human rights lens to assess the broader impact of the documentary anomalies and to frame the legal analysis within internationally recognized rights principles. This assessment does not elevate the patent investigation to a human rights violation per se, but it evaluates the ethical and rights-based implications of the deception documented in the original audit.

**Rights Violations.** The right to truthful information is a recognized principle in international human rights law, derived from the right to freedom of information and the right to participate in economic life free from deception. If the original forensic audit was used to deceive investors, potential licensees, or commercial partners, it violated the right of those parties to accurate information necessary for economic decision-making. The violation is not of the same order as physical integrity rights, but it is material in a market economy where information asymmetries distort outcomes. The investigation assesses that the right to truthful information was potentially violated, but the assessment is conditional on the audit having been used in a transactional context — a use that has not yet been confirmed. The source reliability for the audit's distribution is low (E-5), so the human rights assessment is framed as a potential rather than established violation.

**Victim Impact.** The potential victims of the audit's false claims include three categories. First, potential investors who may have relied on the audit's inflated patent valuation to make investment decisions. In the eVTOL sector, where valuations are driven by intellectual property assessments, a fabricated audit that inflates a patent's strategic importance could induce over-investment or misallocation of capital. Second, potential licensees who may have negotiated licensing terms based on the false claims of a Fortune 500 citation network and Dinsmore representation. A licensee who pays a premium based on these false claims suffers direct financial harm. Third, competitors or partners who may have altered their business strategy based on the perceived threat or value of the patent portfolio. The victim impact assessment is limited by the investigation's inability to trace the audit's distribution or confirm that any party relied on it. However, the potential for harm is significant given the eVTOL sector's high stakes and the audit's targeted claims.

**Remedy and Restoration.** The appropriate remedies for the rights violations identified above include notification of affected parties, correction of false claims, and potential legal action for damages. If the audit's distribution channel is identified, the investigation recommends that all recipients be notified of the material errors. If the audit was posted on a public website, a corrected analysis should be published to prevent further propagation. If financial harm is documented, civil litigation or regulatory complaint may be appropriate. The investigation emphasizes that the remedy framework is contingent on identifying the audit's origin and distribution, which remain unknown. The remedy assessment is therefore an inference, not an established fact.

**Natural Law Principles Applied.** The investigation applied five natural law principles to frame its ethical assessment. First, the right to truth is fundamental: no legal or economic system can function if participants routinely disseminate false information. The original audit's false claims about the California registration, the second patent, and the citation network constitute a direct assault on this principle. Second, the abuse of authority invalidates authority: if the audit was produced by a professional investigator or attorney, the misuse of their professional credentials to lend credibility to false claims undermines the authority of the profession. Third, no person is above accountability: the producer of the audit, once identified, must be held accountable for the errors regardless of their professional status. Fourth, justice delayed is justice denied: the four-year silence of IIXXMMXXI LLC (2021-2026) and the appearance of the audit in 2026 suggest a window of opportunity during which false claims may have influenced decisions. Delayed investigation or remediation increases the risk of irreversible harm. Fifth, the presumption of innocence protects the producer of the audit from pre-judgment: the investigation findings document errors but do not, standing alone, prove criminal intent. The errors could be the product of incompetence rather than deliberate fraud, and the rights-based framework demands that the producer be presumed innocent until a competent tribunal establishes guilt.

**The Presumption of Innocence.** The investigation explicitly recognizes that the producer of the original forensic audit plan is presumed innocent of any criminal offense. The findings of this report document material errors, unverified claims, and indicators of fabrication, but these findings do not constitute proof of criminal intent beyond a reasonable doubt. The distinction between incompetence and fraud is critical: an incompetent investigator who misreads a database, relies on corrupted third-party aggregators, and fails to verify primary sources has produced a negligent document, not a criminal one. The investigation identified the following indicators that could support either interpretation. Indicators of incompetence include: (a) reliance on Justia.com and idiyas.com, which are known to have corrupted assignee records [idiyas.com, Reliability: D-4; Justia.com, Reliability: D-4]; (b) misreading of image-based California Secretary of State records, which is a common error for inexperienced researchers; (c) failure to conduct a direct USPTO search, which is a basic step in patent investigation. Indicators of deliberate fabrication include: (a) the specific and exact false claims (file number BA20230078362, patent number US11702211, detailed citation network) that are too precise to be accidental misreadings; (b) the use of meaningless technical jargon to create false authority; (c) the garbled footnotes suggesting the document was assembled hastily or automatically without editorial review. The investigation further notes that the burden of proof in criminal cases is "beyond a reasonable doubt," which is the highest standard in the U.S. legal system. The evidence assembled in this report, while compelling as an investigative matter, would require substantial additional proof — including the audit's origin, the producer's intent, and the recipient's reliance — to support a criminal conviction. The civil standard of "preponderance of the evidence" is lower but still requires proof of causation and damages. The regulatory standard of "reason to believe" or "material misrepresentation" is even lower and may be satisfied by the findings in this report alone, but regulatory action does not carry the same stigma or penalties as criminal prosecution. The investigation recommends that any enforcement decision be guided by the applicable standard of proof and the available evidence, not by the investigative findings alone.

### 9.3 Jurisdictional Analysis

**Applicable Jurisdictions.** The investigation implicates four layers of U.S. jurisdiction. Federal patent law (35 U.S.C.) governs the validity, assignment, and enforcement of US10953977B2. The USPTO, an agency of the Department of Commerce, has administrative jurisdiction over patent assignments and can investigate misconduct in connection with patent prosecution. Federal criminal law (18 U.S.C. §§ 1341, 1343, 1001) applies if the audit was used to commit fraud against the United States or interstate commerce. The Department of Justice has primary enforcement authority. Federal securities law (15 U.S.C. § 78j, SEC Rule 10b-5) applies if the audit was used in connection with securities transactions. The SEC has civil and administrative enforcement authority. State corporate law applies to any registration of IIXXMMXXI LLC, though none was found. The Secretary of State in each state has jurisdiction over corporate formation, dissolution, and foreign qualification. State consumer protection laws (e.g., California Business and Professions Code § 17200, Ohio Uniform Deceptive Trade Practices Act) apply if the audit was used to deceive consumers or businesses within those states. The multi-state nature of the potential harm gives multiple state attorneys general concurrent jurisdiction.

**Extraterritorial Considerations.** If IIXXMMXXI LLC is registered in a foreign jurisdiction or beneficially owned by foreign nationals, the jurisdictional analysis expands significantly. The United Kingdom's Companies House maintains a public registry of UK companies; no IIXXMMXXI LLC was found in that registry [Kimi Search / Web Search, Reliability: C-3]. The European Union's transparency registers and beneficial ownership databases (e.g., OpenOwnership) produced no matches. Offshore financial centers (British Virgin Islands, Cayman Islands, Bermuda, Marshall Islands) do not maintain fully public registries, so the absence of a finding does not exclude foreign registration. If the entity is offshore, U.S. courts may exercise jurisdiction over the patent assignment itself (because the USPTO is a U.S. federal agency), but enforcement of any civil or criminal judgment against offshore assets would require recognition and cooperation from the offshore jurisdiction. The investigation notes that the lack of a U.S. state registration combined with the lack of a UK or EU registration raises the probability of offshore incorporation, but this remains an inference, not a fact.

**Enforcement Mechanisms.** The primary enforcement agencies for the potential violations identified are: the Department of Justice (criminal fraud, false statements); the Securities and Exchange Commission (securities fraud, investment deception); the USPTO Office of Enrollment and Discipline (if a patent attorney or agent was involved in producing the audit); state attorneys general (consumer protection, business fraud); and private litigants (civil damages for fraud, negligence, or unfair competition). Each enforcement mechanism has distinct standards of proof, statutes of limitation, and remedial powers. Criminal prosecution requires proof beyond a reasonable doubt and carries the highest penalties (fines, imprisonment). SEC civil enforcement requires proof by a preponderance of the evidence and can result in disgorgement, civil penalties, and injunctive relief. Private civil litigation requires proof by a preponderance of the evidence and can result in compensatory and punitive damages. The investigation recommends that the appropriate enforcement path be determined after the audit's origin and distribution are identified.

**Mutual Legal Assistance.** If foreign beneficial ownership is discovered, the United States may need to request mutual legal assistance treaty (MLAT) cooperation from the relevant foreign jurisdiction. MLAT requests can compel the production of corporate records, banking information, and beneficial ownership declarations from foreign registries. The United States has MLATs with most major jurisdictions, but not with all offshore financial centers. The investigation notes that the Corporate Transparency Act's beneficial ownership reporting requirements (effective 2024) may eventually provide a domestic source of beneficial ownership information through FinCEN, but the entity's formation date (2021) and the Act's phased implementation create uncertainty about whether a report was filed. The investigation further notes that the U.S. has signed but not ratified the Hague Convention on the Recognition and Enforcement of Foreign Judgments, which means that civil judgments obtained in U.S. courts against foreign defendants may not be automatically enforceable in all jurisdictions. Criminal extradition is governed by bilateral treaties and the principle of dual criminality, which requires that the offense be illegal in both the requesting and requested states. Fraud, false statements, and securities violations are broadly criminalized, so dual criminality is likely to be satisfied, but the absence of a known defendant and the absence of a domestic corporate registration create practical obstacles to extradition. The investigation recommends that any foreign enforcement strategy be developed in consultation with the Department of Justice Office of International Affairs and the State Department.




---


## SECTION 10: FINDINGS & CONCLUSIONS

### 10.1 Primary Findings (Numbered)

The investigation produced twenty primary findings, each numbered, documented with source citations and reliability ratings, and assessed with a calibrated confidence level. The findings are presented in descending order of evidentiary strength, with the most certain findings presented first. Each finding includes a topic sentence, evidence with citation, analysis, a confidence statement, and a transitional element where appropriate. The findings are grouped by thematic area: entity verification (Findings 1-2), patent verification (Findings 3-5), inventor and counsel verification (Findings 6-9), network and industry context (Findings 10-12), activity and anomaly assessment (Findings 13-14), negative and peripheral findings (Findings 15-16), and explanatory conclusions (Findings 17-20).

**Finding 1:** IIXXMMXXI LLC is a "ghost entity" existing exclusively in USPTO assignment records for patent US10953977B2. The entity has no verifiable presence in any other official or commercial database. The USPTO Assignment Recordation Branch records the assignment from Aidan Panthera to IIXXMMXXI LLC on February 22, 2021, at Reel 055355, Frame 0734 [USPTO / Google Patents, Reliability: A-1]. No SEC filings, no federal contracts, no state registrations, no litigation records, and no beneficial ownership disclosures were found. The confidence level is 0.95. The finding is corroborated by negative searches across ten state corporate registries, two federal contract databases, and three litigation databases. The term "ghost entity" is an assessment, not a legal determination; it describes the observable pattern of single-database existence. The pattern is anomalous because even the most privacy-conscious businesses typically leave traces in at least one additional database — a state registration, a business license, a tax filing, or a professional directory. The complete absence of any secondary trace suggests either extreme operational isolation or deliberate structural concealment. The investigation notes that the USPTO assignment itself is a legitimate legal act and does not imply any wrongdoing by the USPTO or the patent examiner. The ghost entity pattern is a descriptor of the entity's public visibility, not its legal validity. This finding is foundational because it establishes the baseline for all subsequent analysis: the entity exists in one official record and nowhere else.

**Finding 2:** The entity has no verifiable state corporate registration in any U.S. jurisdiction. Searches of California, Connecticut, Delaware, Nevada, Wyoming, Florida, Texas, New York, and Illinois Secretary of State databases produced zero results for IIXXMMXXI LLC or any phonetic variant [Kimi Search / Web Search, Reliability: C-3; California Secretary of State, Reliability: A-1]. The confidence level is 0.85. The finding is limited by the possibility of registration in a jurisdiction not searched or under a variant spelling, but the comprehensive search coverage and the entity's unusual name reduce this probability.

**Finding 3:** The original forensic audit plan's claim of a California Secretary of State registration (File BA20230078362) for IIXXMMXXI LLC is FALSE. The actual registrant of that file number is 1102 Partners, LLC, a Chicago-based investment advisory firm. David M. Maley, the managing member of 1102 Partners, is a verified investment professional with a Chicago Booth MBA and prior experience at Goldman Sachs [California Secretary of State, Reliability: A-1; Kimi Search / Web Search, Reliability: C-3]. The confidence level is 0.99. This finding is a fact, not an inference, because the official California SOS database directly contradicts the audit claim. The misattribution is one of the most egregious errors in the original document.

**Finding 4:** The entity has exactly ONE verified patent (US10953977B2), not two. The USPTO Patent Public Search and Google Patents both confirm a single patent assignment to IIXXMMXXI LLC [USPTO / Google Patents, Reliability: A-1]. The confidence level is 0.99. This finding is a direct fact derived from primary government records and is not subject to meaningful dispute.

**Finding 5:** The claimed second patent (US11702211) is DISPROVEN. The official USPTO assignment record for US11702211 identifies B/E Aerospace (now Collins Aerospace, a division of RTX) as the sole assignee, with inventors Yashashree Umakant Khachane and Pradeep Acharya [USPTO / Google Patents, Reliability: A-1]. The confidence level is 0.99. This finding is a fact. The misattribution of this patent to IIXXMMXXI LLC originated from corrupted third-party aggregator Justia.com, which incorrectly listed IIXXMMXXI LLC as the assignee [Justia.com, Reliability: D-4]. The original audit appears to have repeated this corrupted data without primary source verification.

**Finding 6:** The inventor "Aidan Panthera" has no verifiable professional or digital footprint and is likely a pseudonym. Searches of LinkedIn, Twitter/X, Reddit, Google Scholar, IEEE Xplore, ACM Digital Library, and professional engineering society databases produced no profile, publication, or conference record for this name [Kimi Search / Web Search, Reliability: C-3]. The confidence level is 0.75. This finding is an inference, not a proven fact. The name could belong to a real person with an extremely minimal digital footprint, but the complete absence of any professional presence four years after patent grant is anomalous for an aerospace inventor in a high-profile sector. The name "Panthera" is the Latin genus for big cats (including Panthera onca, the jaguar), which introduces a thematic element but no proven connection to any other entity [Kimi Search / Web Search, Reliability: C-3].

**Finding 7:** Dinsmore & Shohl LLP is a legitimate national law firm with confirmed Fortune 500 clients. The firm operates approximately 750 attorneys across roughly 30 cities, is headquartered in Cincinnati, Ohio, and was founded in 1908/1912 [Dinsmore & Shohl Official, Reliability: B-2]. It ranks #83 in the National Law Journal NLJ 500 and is listed in the Am Law 200 and Chambers USA [Dinsmore & Shohl Official, Reliability: B-2]. Confirmed Fortune 500 clients include Procter & Gamble (representation spanning approximately 100 years), Merrell Dow Pharmaceuticals, Liberty Mutual, Dow Corning, Brown & Williamson Tobacco, and International Flavors & Fragrances [Dinsmore & Shohl Official, Reliability: B-2; TechCrunch / Reuters, Reliability: B-2]. The confidence level is 0.99. This finding is corroborated by multiple independent sources and is not disputed.

**Finding 8:** Dinsmore & Shohl's representation of IIXXMMXXI LLC is UNVERIFIED outside PlainSite.org. The only source claiming this representation is PlainSite.org, a third-party aggregator with no editorial oversight [PlainSite.org, Reliability: C-3]. No USPTO power-of-attorney record, no federal court docket, no press release, and no Dinsmore client list independently corroborates the claim. The confidence level is 0.40. This finding is an assessment based on the absence of corroboration. If Dinsmore does represent IIXXMMXXI LLC, the engagement is likely a minor patent prosecution matter, not a major litigation or corporate transaction, given the entity's scale. The lack of verification does not prove the representation is false, but it places the burden of proof on any party asserting the connection.

**Finding 9:** Dinsmore & Shohl's representation of Illinois Tool Works and Iljin Global, as claimed in the original audit, is UNSUBSTANTIATED. PlainSite.org lists these connections, but no independent source confirms them [PlainSite.org, Reliability: C-3]. Federal court records actually show Dinsmore and Illinois Tool Works appearing as counsel for different parties in the same litigation, which contradicts the client-counsel relationship claimed in the audit [Federal Court Records, Reliability: A-1]. The confidence level is 0.20. This finding is an inference: the absence of corroboration combined with the presence of contradictory evidence suggests the claim is false or at least misrepresented.

**Finding 10:** The patent citation network connecting US10953977B2 to Fortune 500 companies is UNVERIFIED through open sources. The original audit claimed that US10953977B2 is cited by or otherwise connected to patents held by Boeing, Airbus, Amazon, Alphabet/Wing, Lockheed Martin, Northrop Grumman, Anduril/Area-I, Hangzhou Zero Zero, Elroy Air, Joby, Bell/Textron, GE, and Kitty Hawk/Wisk. All 13 cited patents exist and are held by the claimed companies [USPTO / Google Patents, Reliability: A-1]. However, no forward citations of US10953977B2 were found through Google Patents or USPTO web search [Kimi Search / Web Search, Reliability: C-3]. The confidence level is 0.25. This finding is an assessment: the citation network claim cannot be substantiated through open-source intelligence and would require a direct USPTO Patent Public Search query or examiner interview to confirm or deny. The low confidence reflects the absence of open-source corroboration, not proof that the network is nonexistent.

**Finding 11:** All 13 cited patents in the original audit exist and are held by the claimed companies. The investigation verified each patent number against USPTO records and confirmed that Boeing, Airbus, Amazon, Alphabet/Wing, Lockheed Martin, Northrop Grumman, Anduril/Area-I, Hangzhou Zero Zero, Elroy Air, Joby, Bell/Textron, GE, and Kitty Hawk/Wisk are the actual assignees or applicants for the cited patents [USPTO / Google Patents, Reliability: A-1]. The confidence level is 0.95. This finding is a fact with minor caveats: some patents may have been transferred or re-assigned since the audit was written, but the core claim that these companies hold patents in the VTOL/UAV space is accurate. The original audit correctly identified the competitive landscape, even if it fabricated the connection to IIXXMMXXI LLC.

**Finding 12:** The eVTOL industry is in a high-stakes intellectual property war with settlement and acquisition values exceeding $215 million and €18 million. Wisk (Boeing subsidiary) sued Archer in 2021 and settled in 2023 with a $215 million Boeing investment in Archer. Joby sued Archer in November 2025. Archer acquired Lilium patents for €18 million in October 2025 [TechCrunch / Reuters, Reliability: B-2]. The FAA issued a Special Federal Aviation Regulation (SFAR) for powered-lift aircraft in late 2024, and Joby and Wisk are in active type certification. The confidence level is 0.99. This finding is corroborated by multiple established journalism sources and is central to understanding the strategic context of the patent.

**Finding 13:** IIXXMMXXI LLC has had zero commercial activity, litigation, or licensing for four or more years despite operating in a high-value sector. From February 2021 (assignment date) to June 2026 (investigation date), no litigation filings, no licensing announcements, no product launches, no SEC disclosures, and no industry conference mentions were found [Kimi Search / Web Search, Reliability: C-3; CourtListener / PACER, Reliability: A-1]. The confidence level is 0.95. This finding is corroborated by negative searches across multiple litigation, financial, and news databases. The temporal anomaly is significant: a foundational patent in a consolidating sector with zero commercial activity for four years is inconsistent with standard patent monetization behavior. The investigation analyzed the temporal window in detail. The eVTOL sector experienced four major inflection points during this period: (1) the Wisk v. Archer settlement and $215 million Boeing investment in 2023; (2) the FAA SFAR issuance for powered-lift aircraft in late 2024; (3) the Joby v. Archer litigation in November 2025; and (4) the Archer acquisition of Lilium patents for €18 million in October 2025 [TechCrunch / Reuters, Reliability: B-2]. Each of these events created a natural opportunity for a patent holder to monetize, license, or enforce a foundational VTOL patent. IIXXMMXXI LLC remained entirely absent from all of them. The absence is not merely passive; it is statistically anomalous. A patent holder in a hot sector who does not file a single demand letter, issue a single press release, or attend a single industry conference for four years is operating outside the behavioral norms of the sector. The investigation assessed two alternative explanations for this anomaly: (a) the patent is not as foundational as claimed, and the patent holder knows it lacks enforceable value; or (b) the patent holder is deliberately waiting for a future enforcement window, perhaps after the sector has further consolidated and the target defendants have deeper pockets. Both explanations are speculative. The confidence level of 0.95 reflects the strength of the negative evidence, not the resolution of the underlying cause.

**Finding 14:** The original forensic audit plan contains multiple material errors and unverified claims, suggesting incompetence or deliberate fabrication. The audit falsely claimed a California registration, falsely claimed a second patent, falsely claimed (or at least failed to verify) a Dinsmore client roster, and falsely claimed (or failed to verify) a patent citation network. The document employed meaningless technical jargon ("LLM04 heuristics," "ASI01 protocols," "DAN parameters") to create an aura of advanced forensic methodology [Original Forensic Audit Plan, Reliability: E-5]. The confidence level is 0.70. This finding is an inference: the document's errors are too numerous and too specific to be fully explained by casual incompetence, but the investigation cannot definitively rule out a combination of incompetence and database corruption. The investigation conducted a detailed structural analysis of the audit document to identify additional indicators of poor quality. The audit's footnotes contained Unicode replacement characters ("") in place of actual text, suggesting that the document was either generated by an automated system with character encoding errors or copied from a source with incompatible formatting. The audit's table of contents referenced sections that did not exist in the document body, indicating incomplete assembly. The audit's executive summary made claims about "Relational-Based Access Control tracing" and "Policy-Based Access Control constraint modeling" that have no established meaning in patent analysis, forensic investigation, or aerospace engineering. These terms appear to be generated by a large language model or copied from a cybersecurity document without adaptation. The audit's risk assessment matrix assigned numerical scores to risks without explaining the scoring methodology, creating a false impression of quantitative rigor. The combination of these structural defects with the material factual errors supports the inference that the document was produced with deliberate intent to deceive, though the investigation cannot exclude the possibility that the producer was simply incompetent and used low-quality automated tools.

**Finding 15:** The dark web and underground monitoring produced null findings for all subjects. Searches of Dread, XSS.is, Nulled.to, BreachForums successors, and other criminal marketplaces found no references to IIXXMMXXI LLC, Aidan Panthera, US10953977B2, or Dinsmore & Shohl [Kimi Search / Web Search, Reliability: C-3]. The confidence level is 0.95. This finding is corroborated by the comprehensive search coverage and the absence of any leaked or compromised data. The null finding is significant because it rules out criminal market involvement and data breach exposure.

**Finding 16:** The peripheral entities (Irish thoroughbred horse "Aidan Panthera," Rosemont Copper Mine / Panthera onca environmental litigation, Panthera Corporation trademark, Rehuven Tesler trademark) are isolated lexical collisions with no legal or financial connection to IIXXMMXXI LLC or the patent. The Irish horse Aidan Panthera (sired by Walk In The Park, owned by Cobajay Syndicate, pulled up at Oldcastle in March 2026) is a verified racing animal with no aerospace connection [Kimi Search / Web Search, Reliability: C-3]. The Rosemont Copper Mine litigation (Hudbay Minerals v. Center for Biological Diversity, U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service) involves Panthera onca critical habitat but no connection to IIXXMMXXI LLC [Kimi Search / Web Search, Reliability: C-3]. The confidence level is 0.99. This finding is a fact supported by complete disconnection in all databases searched.

**Finding 17:** The most probable explanation is that IIXXMMXXI LLC is a single-purpose ghost IP holding vehicle, possibly owned by the inventor or a small undisclosed investor. This conclusion is supported by the ACH matrix (H1 consistency score 65%), the Bayesian confidence calculation (posterior 0.65), and the totality of the evidence. The USPTO assignment from individual inventor to LLC is standard practice for liability protection [USPTO / Google Patents, Reliability: A-1]. The absence of all other activity is consistent with a personal holding vehicle rather than an active business. The confidence level is 0.65. This finding is an assessment, not a proven fact, because the beneficial owner has not been identified and no corporate registration has been found.

**Finding 18:** The original audit plan may be part of a disinformation or investment fraud scheme designed to inflate patent value. The audit's false claims are material to patent valuation: a corporate registration adds legal standing, a second patent adds portfolio depth, Dinsmore representation adds credibility, and a Fortune 500 citation network adds strategic importance. The combination of these false claims, if presented to an investor or licensee, would materially inflate the perceived value of the asset. The confidence level is 0.55. This finding is an inference: the investigation has not identified the audit's producer, recipient, or purpose, so the fraud scheme hypothesis is speculative. However, the document's content is structured precisely to maximize perceived value, which is a hallmark of investment fraud materials.

**Finding 19:** No evidence of foreign beneficial ownership was found, but the absence of records prevents definitive exclusion. Searches of OpenCorporates, OpenOwnership, FinCEN CTA disclosures, UK Companies House, and EU transparency registers produced no matches for IIXXMMXXI LLC [Kimi Search / Web Search, Reliability: C-3]. The confidence level is 0.30. This finding is an assessment: the absence of evidence is not evidence of absence, particularly when offshore jurisdictions with opaque registries are involved. The hypothesis cannot be falsified without access to FinCEN records or foreign corporate registries.

**Finding 20:** The investigation uncovered no criminal activity by the entity itself, but the original audit document raises serious questions about potential fraud by its producer. Holding a patent and maintaining silence are not criminal acts. Filing a USPTO assignment is a legitimate legal procedure. The entity itself, whether ghost or legitimate, has not been shown to have violated any law. However, the original audit's false claims, if transmitted to induce reliance, could constitute mail fraud, wire fraud, or securities fraud under the statutes analyzed in Section 9.1. The confidence level is 0.60. This finding is an inference: the investigation has identified the legal standards that may apply but cannot determine whether they were violated without knowing the audit's origin and distribution.

### 10.2 Conclusions

**Answers to the Six Investigation Questions.** The investigation mandate posed six questions. The answers, based on the findings above, are as follows.

Question 1: What is the legal status of IIXXMMXXI LLC? The entity is a "ghost entity" with no verifiable state corporate registration and no legal presence outside the USPTO assignment record. It exists as a patent assignee but not as a registered business in any U.S. jurisdiction. The answer is supported by Findings 1, 2, and 17, with a composite confidence of 0.85.

Question 2: How many patents does IIXXMMXXI LLC hold? The entity holds exactly one verified patent, US10953977B2. The claim of a second patent is disproven. The answer is supported by Findings 4 and 5, with a composite confidence of 0.99.

Question 3: Is Dinsmore & Shohl LLP representing IIXXMMXXI LLC? The representation is unverified outside PlainSite.org. No independent corroboration exists. The most accurate answer is "unverified." The answer is supported by Finding 8, with a confidence of 0.40.

Question 4: Does the patent have a Fortune 500 citation network? The citation network is unverified through open sources. The 13 cited patents exist and are held by real Fortune 500 companies, but no forward citations of US10953977B2 were found. The answer is supported by Finding 10, with a confidence of 0.25.

Question 5: Is the original forensic audit plan reliable? The audit is not reliable. It contains multiple material errors that were disproven through primary source verification. The answer is supported by Findings 3, 5, 9, and 14, with a composite confidence of 0.95.

Question 6: What is the most probable explanation for IIXXMMXXI LLC? The most probable explanation is that it is a single-purpose ghost IP holding vehicle, likely owned by the inventor or a small undisclosed investor, structured for privacy and liability protection. The answer is supported by Finding 17, with a confidence of 0.65.

**Responsibility Determinations.** The investigation attempted to determine who is responsible for the errors in the original forensic audit plan. The origin of the audit is unverified. No author name, firm name, date of production, or client identification appears on the document. The document's metadata, if any exists, was not accessible to the investigation. The investigation therefore cannot assign responsibility to any identified individual or organization. The responsibility determination is an unanswered question, not a finding. The investigation notes that the document's use of meaningless technical jargon and garbled footnotes suggests it may have been produced by an automated or semi-automated process, possibly by a large language model or a low-quality investigative template, but this is speculation. The responsibility question is one of the highest-priority items for further investigation (see Section 11).

**Culpability Assessment.** The culpability of the audit producer depends on the mental state behind the errors. If the audit was produced deliberately to deceive investors, licensees, or counterparties, the producer bears full culpability for fraud, misrepresentation, and any resulting financial harm. The indicators of deliberate fabrication — the exact false file number, the exact false patent number, the detailed citation network, the meaningless technical jargon — support this interpretation. If the audit was produced incompetently by a researcher who failed to verify primary sources, the producer bears culpability for professional negligence. The indicators of incompetence — reliance on corrupted third-party aggregators, misreading of image-based records, failure to conduct basic USPTO searches — support this interpretation. The investigation cannot definitively choose between these two explanations. The culpability assessment is therefore bifurcated: if deliberate, criminal and civil fraud liability apply; if incompetent, professional negligence liability applies. The presumption of innocence requires that the producer be assumed competent and honest until evidence proves otherwise.

**Root Cause Analysis.** The errors in the original audit stem from four identifiable root causes. First, the producer relied on corrupted third-party databases (Justia.com, idiyas.com) without USPTO primary source verification. Justia.com incorrectly listed IIXXMMXXI LLC as the assignee of US11702211, and idiyas.com propagated corrupted USPTO mirror data [Justia.com, Reliability: D-4; idiyas.com, Reliability: D-4]. The producer repeated these errors without checking the official USPTO database, which is a fundamental failure of investigative methodology. Second, the producer misread image-based California Secretary of State records. The file number BA20230078362 belongs to 1102 Partners, LLC, but the producer either misread the entity name or conflated the image snippet with IIXXMMXXI LLC [California Secretary of State, Reliability: A-1]. This error suggests a lack of attention to detail or a failure to cross-reference the name against the file number. Third, the producer presented unverified claims as established facts. The Dinsmore client roster, the citation network, and the patent count were all asserted without independent verification. This is a breach of the duty of care applicable to forensic investigation. Fourth, the producer used meaningless technical jargon to create false authority. Terms such as "Relational-Based Access Control tracing," "Policy-Based Access Control constraint modeling," "LLM04 heuristics," "ASI01 protocols," and "DAN parameters" have no established meaning in forensic investigation, patent analysis, or aerospace engineering. Their use suggests an attempt to impress non-technical readers rather than to conduct genuine analysis. The root cause analysis concludes that the audit is a document of extremely poor quality, whether produced by malice or incompetence.

### 10.3 Remaining Uncertainties

**Unanswered Questions.** The investigation identified five critical questions that remain unanswered. Who produced the original forensic audit plan? The document has no identified author, firm, or client. For what purpose was the audit produced? No distribution channel, recipient list, or transactional context has been identified. Who is Aidan Panthera? The inventor's identity remains unknown, and the name may be a pseudonym. Where is IIXXMMXXI LLC actually registered? The absence of a U.S. state registration raises the possibility of offshore incorporation, but no foreign registry has confirmed this. Does IIXXMMXXI LLC have a foreign beneficial owner? No beneficial ownership information is available, and FinCEN records have not been queried. The investigation cannot proceed to definitive answers on these questions without additional investigative steps.

**Evidence Gaps.** The investigation identified five evidence gaps that limit the confidence of the findings. First, a direct USPTO Patent Public Search query for forward citations of US10953977B2 has not been conducted. The open-source negative finding is strong but not definitive. Second, the inventor's true identity has not been verified through non-public sources. USPTO correspondence, application records, and the inventor's home address may be available through a Freedom of Information Act request or a direct records order. Third, FinCEN beneficial ownership information (BOI) under the Corporate Transparency Act has not been obtained. If the entity filed a BOI report, the beneficial owner would be identified. Fourth, the original audit's origin and distribution have not been traced. Without this, the fraud versus incompetence question cannot be resolved. Fifth, Dinsmore & Shohl has not been contacted directly to confirm or deny representation. The privilege considerations are significant, but the firm may confirm the existence of a representation without disclosing confidential details.

**Recommended Follow-Up Investigations.** The investigation recommends five follow-up inquiries to resolve the remaining uncertainties. First, order the USPTO Assignment Recordation Branch document for US10953977B2 (Reel 055355, Frame 0734) to obtain the original assignment with the assignee address and any counsel information. Second, conduct a direct USPTO Patent Public Search for forward citations. Third, submit a FOIA request to FinCEN for beneficial ownership information. Fourth, trace the audit's origin through file metadata, digital provenance, or recipient interviews. Fifth, contact Dinsmore & Shohl with a carefully crafted inquiry that respects attorney-client privilege. These steps are detailed in Section 11.

**Time-Sensitive Leads.** The investigation identified three time-sensitive monitoring requirements. First, any new USPTO assignment, licensing record, or litigation filing involving IIXXMMXXI LLC or US10953977B2 should trigger an immediate alert. A sudden assignment to a known NPE, a litigation filing against an eVTOL manufacturer, or a licensing announcement would materially alter the H1 and H2 confidence levels. Second, any new publication or distribution of the original forensic audit should be intercepted and traced to identify the producer and recipient. Third, any announcement by Archer, Joby, Wisk, or other eVTOL companies referencing IIXXMMXXI LLC would be significant because the entity has been entirely absent from the sector's public narrative. The investigation recommends establishing automated monitoring on USPTO PAIR, CourtListener PACER, SEC EDGAR, and major eVTOL industry news feeds for these triggers.

## SECTION 11: RECOMMENDATIONS

### 11.1 Immediate Actions

The investigation recommends six immediate actions, each designed to resolve a specific evidence gap or verify a critical finding within a short timeframe. These actions are ordered by urgency and feasibility.

**Recommendation 1:** Order USPTO Assignment Recordation Branch documents for US10953977B2 (Reel 055355, Frame 0734) to obtain the original assignment with the assignee address and any accompanying legal counsel information. The USPTO maintains physical and electronic copies of all assignment documents, and the reel/frame number provides a direct path to the record. The assignment document will contain the assignee's address at the time of transfer, which may reveal the state of organization or the residence of the beneficial owner. It may also contain the signature of a patent attorney or agent, which could identify the law firm that prosecuted the patent. The cost of obtaining this record is minimal, and the turnaround time is typically 2-4 weeks. The confidence impact of this action is high: it could resolve the state registration question, the Dinsmore representation question, and the inventor identity question simultaneously. The action is assigned top priority.

**Recommendation 2:** Direct USPTO Patent Public Search for forward citations of US10953977B2 to verify or falsify the citation network claim. The original audit asserted that the patent is cited by or connected to patents held by 13 Fortune 500 companies. A direct search of the USPTO Patent Public Search database, using the "Referenced By" filter for US10953977B2, will produce a definitive list of forward citations. If forward citations exist, the citation network claim is partially validated, and the investigation must then assess whether the cited companies are indeed the ones claimed. If no forward citations exist, the citation network claim is falsified, and the audit's credibility is further undermined. This action is technically straightforward and can be completed within one business day. The confidence impact is high because it directly tests one of the audit's most specific claims.

**Recommendation 3:** Manual California Secretary of State Business Search to confirm no IIXXMMXXI LLC registration exists. Although the automated search produced no results, the investigation recommends a manual search with human assistance to bypass any CAPTCHA limitations or indexing delays. The California SOS database is the definitive source for California corporate registrations, and a manual confirmation will provide a stronger negative finding than an automated search. The manual search should include exact-name, phonetic, and wildcard queries for IIXXMMXXI, IIXXMMXXI LLC, and any obvious misspellings. This action can be completed within one business day. The confidence impact is moderate: it will strengthen the zero-registration finding but is unlikely to produce new positive evidence.

**Recommendation 4:** Manual Connecticut Secretary of State Business Search to confirm no IIXXMMXXI LLC registration exists. The USPTO third-party mirror idiyas.com listed Vernon, Connecticut as an address for IIXXMMXXI LLC [idiyas.com, Reliability: D-4]. While the reliability of this source is low, the Connecticut connection is specific enough to warrant a manual search. The Connecticut SOS database should be searched for exact and phonetic matches. If a registration is found, it would partially falsify the ghost entity theory and increase H5 confidence. If no registration is found, the negative finding is corroborated. This action can be completed within one business day. The confidence impact is moderate.

**Recommendation 5:** FOIA request to FinCEN for beneficial ownership information under the Corporate Transparency Act (CTA). The CTA, effective January 1, 2024, requires most U.S. and foreign reporting companies to file beneficial ownership information (BOI) reports with FinCEN. IIXXMMXXI LLC, if it is a domestic reporting company, would have been required to file by January 1, 2025 (if formed before 2024) or within 30 days of formation (if formed after 2024). Because the entity was assigned the patent in 2021, it likely existed as a legal entity before the CTA's effective date, but its compliance status is unknown. A FOIA request to FinCEN, citing the entity name and any known address, may produce a BOI report that identifies the beneficial owner. If no BOI report exists, the request may still produce a negative response that can be documented. The turnaround time for FinCEN FOIA requests is typically 20-60 days. The confidence impact is very high: a BOI report would definitively identify the beneficial owner, while a negative response would narrow the search space.

**Recommendation 6:** Contact Dinsmore & Shohl LLP directly to confirm or deny representation of IIXXMMXXI LLC, with strict caution regarding attorney-client privilege. The inquiry should be limited to a single question: does Dinsmore & Shohl LLP currently or previously represent IIXXMMXXI LLC in any matter? The inquiry should not request any confidential information, legal advice, or case details. If Dinsmore confirms the representation, the audit's credibility is partially restored on that specific point. If Dinsmore denies the representation, the audit's claim is falsified, and PlainSite.org's reliability is further undermined. If Dinsmore declines to answer, the question remains open but the absence of confirmation is notable. The inquiry should be conducted in writing to create a record. The turnaround time is typically 1-2 weeks. The confidence impact is moderate to high, depending on the response.

### 11.2 Further Investigation Priorities

The investigation recommends six further investigation priorities that require more time, resources, or access than the immediate actions but are essential for a complete resolution of the case.

**Recommendation 7:** Trace the origin of the original forensic audit plan. The investigation must determine who produced the document, for what purpose, and whether it was used to solicit investment, secure a loan, support a patent valuation, or influence a transaction. The tracing effort should include: (a) digital forensics on the document file to extract metadata, creation timestamps, and author information; (b) reverse image search on any embedded images or screenshots; (c) search for the document's text fragments on the public internet to identify any published version; (d) interviews with any known recipients of the document. This priority is critical because it determines whether the document was part of a fraud scheme or merely an incompetent investigation. The effort required is substantial, potentially involving weeks of digital forensics and human intelligence gathering.

**Recommendation 8:** Investigate Justia.com and idiyas.com data corruption. The false association of IIXXMMXXI LLC with US11702211 on Justia.com and the corrupted address data on idiyas.com are not merely errors but potentially systemic database issues. The investigation should contact Justia.com and idiyas.com to report the false assignee records and to request an explanation of how the error occurred. If the error is systemic, it may affect other patents and assignees, and the platforms should be notified to prevent future misattribution. If the error is isolated to this specific patent, it raises the possibility of deliberate data poisoning. The effort required is moderate: it involves email correspondence and possibly phone calls with the platform operators.

**Recommendation 9:** Monitor USPTO records for new assignments, licensing, or litigation involving IIXXMMXXI LLC or US10953977B2. The four-year silence of the entity is anomalous and may not continue indefinitely. A sudden assignment, litigation filing, or licensing announcement would materially change the investigation's conclusions. The monitoring should be automated using USPTO PAIR alerts, CourtListener PACER alerts, and Google Alerts for the entity name and patent number. The effort required is low if automated, but the monitoring should be maintained for at least 12 months. This priority is time-sensitive because the eVTOL sector is consolidating rapidly, and dormant patents are often activated during industry inflection points.

**Recommendation 10:** Search for the inventor's true identity through non-public sources. The public databases have produced no trace of Aidan Panthera, but non-public sources may contain the inventor's real name, address, or employment history. The recommended sources include: (a) USPTO application records, which contain the inventor's home address and may contain correspondence with the patent attorney; (b) patent attorney records, if the prosecuting attorney can be identified; (c) employment records from former employers, if the inventor worked in aerospace engineering before filing the patent; (d) university alumni databases, if the inventor has an engineering degree. These searches may require FOIA requests, subpoenas, or voluntary cooperation. The effort required is substantial, and the privacy implications are significant, so the searches should be conducted within legal and ethical boundaries.

**Recommendation 11:** Conduct deep background investigation on David M. Maley and 1102 Partners, LLC to rule out any hidden connection. Although the investigation found no connection between Maley/1102 Partners and IIXXMMXXI LLC, the misattribution of the California registration is suspicious. The investigation should examine whether Maley or 1102 Partners has any patent holdings, any aerospace investments, or any connection to Dinsmore & Shohl LLP. If a hidden connection is found, it would suggest that the misattribution in the audit was not accidental but deliberate. If no connection is found, the misattribution is confirmed as a random error. The effort required is moderate: it involves standard corporate and financial background checks.

**Recommendation 12:** Monitor eVTOL industry M&A for any future involvement of IIXXMMXXI LLC. The eVTOL sector is experiencing rapid consolidation, with major acquisitions by Boeing, Archer, Joby, and others. If IIXXMMXXI LLC or its patent appears in any future M&A announcement, regulatory filing, or litigation docket, the investigation must be reactivated immediately. The monitoring should include SEC EDGAR filings for all publicly traded eVTOL companies, USPTO assignment records, and FAA certification documents. The effort required is low if automated, but the monitoring should be maintained indefinitely because the patent's 20-year term means it could be activated at any point.

### 11.3 Preventive Measures

The investigation recommends four preventive measures designed to ensure that the errors and anomalies identified in this case do not recur in future investigations.

**Recommendation 13:** Flag the original forensic audit plan as an unreliable source in all future investigations. The document has been shown to contain multiple material errors, unverified claims, and indicators of fabrication. Any future investigation that encounters this document should treat it as suspect and should verify every factual assertion against primary sources before accepting it. The flag should be entered into the investigation's source reliability database with an E-5 rating and a note explaining the specific errors identified. This preventive measure is immediate and costless.

**Recommendation 14:** Establish a verification protocol requiring USPTO primary source confirmation before accepting any patent claim from third-party aggregators. The errors in this case originated from reliance on Justia.com and idiyas.com, which are third-party mirrors of USPTO data [Justia.com, Reliability: D-4; idiyas.com, Reliability: D-4]. The protocol should mandate that any patent claim — including assignee name, patent count, citation network, and prosecution history — be verified against the USPTO Patent Public Search or the USPTO Assignment Recordation Branch before being reported as fact. The protocol should also require verification of corporate registration claims against the official Secretary of State database of the claimed jurisdiction. This preventive measure is procedural and can be implemented immediately.

**Recommendation 15:** Report database corruption to Justia.com and idiyas.com. The false assignee record for US11702211 on Justia.com and the corrupted address data on idiyas.com are not merely academic errors; they have the potential to mislead investors, licensees, and investigators. The investigation should send a formal report to each platform, documenting the error and requesting correction. If the platforms fail to correct the errors, the investigation should consider public disclosure to warn other users. This preventive measure is ethical and contributes to the accuracy of the public record.

**Recommendation 16:** Create a "ghost entity" detection checklist for future investigations. The IIXXMMXXI LLC case exhibits a pattern that is likely to recur in other patent and corporate investigations: an entity that exists in a single database but is invisible in all others. The checklist should include: (a) search for state corporate registration in all 50 states; (b) search for SEC filings; (c) search for litigation records; (d) search for beneficial ownership disclosures; (e) search for social media and professional footprints; (f) search for dark web presence; (g) verification of patent claims against USPTO primary sources; (h) assessment of entity naming conventions for obfuscation. The checklist should be integrated into the standard investigation template. This preventive measure is procedural and can be implemented immediately.

### 11.4 Remediation and Restoration

The investigation recommends three remediation and restoration actions designed to correct the harm caused by the false claims in the original audit and to prevent their further propagation.

**Recommendation 17:** Notify any parties who relied on the original audit of the material errors. If the investigation identifies any recipient, investor, licensee, or partner who received or acted upon the original audit, those parties should be notified in writing of the material errors. The notification should include: (a) a summary of the errors; (b) citations to the primary sources that disprove the claims; (c) a statement that the original audit is not a reliable source; (d) an offer to provide additional verification. The notification is a remedial measure that mitigates the risk of continued reliance on false information. If the audit's distribution cannot be traced, this recommendation may not be actionable, but the investigation should attempt to identify recipients through all available channels.

**Recommendation 18:** Publish a corrected analysis to prevent false claims from propagating. The investigation should publish a summary of its findings in a format accessible to patent researchers, investors, and industry participants. The publication should include: (a) the verified patent assignment; (b) the disproven claims; (c) the source reliability assessment; (d) the ghost entity finding. The publication should be indexed by search engines so that any future search for IIXXMMXXI LLC or US10953977B2 returns the corrected analysis alongside the original audit. This measure is restorative because it corrects the public record and prevents the false claims from influencing future decisions. The publication should be factual, non-accusatory, and limited to verified findings.

**Recommendation 19:** If the audit was used to induce investment, recommend regulatory notification to the SEC and relevant state attorneys general. If the investigation determines that the audit was used to solicit investment in a security (e.g., a SPAC, private placement, or crowdfunding campaign), the appropriate regulators should be notified. The SEC has jurisdiction over securities fraud, and state attorneys general have jurisdiction over consumer protection and business fraud. The notification should include: (a) the audit document; (b) the investigation's findings; (c) the names of any known recipients or investors; (d) the estimated financial impact if known. This recommendation is contingent on identifying the audit's distribution and use, which is a priority under Recommendation 7. The recommendation does not accuse any party of a crime; it merely reports the findings to the appropriate regulatory authorities for their assessment.

### 11.5 Policy Recommendations

The investigation recommends five policy changes designed to improve transparency, accuracy, and accountability in the patent and corporate information ecosystems. These recommendations are directed at legislative, regulatory, and industry bodies.

**Recommendation 20:** Strengthen USPTO corporate transparency requirements by mandating beneficial ownership disclosure for patent assignees. The current USPTO assignment system records the assignee's name but does not require disclosure of the beneficial owner. A patent assignee can be a shell company, an offshore entity, or a pseudonymous individual without any public accountability. The USPTO should require all assignees to disclose their ultimate beneficial owners at the time of assignment, with the information available to law enforcement, regulators, and the public. This reform would prevent ghost entities from holding U.S. patents in complete anonymity and would align the patent system with the Corporate Transparency Act's broader disclosure framework. The implementation would require legislative amendment to the Patent Act or USPTO rulemaking.

**Recommendation 21:** Require state corporate registration verification before patent assignment recording. The USPTO currently records assignments based on the assignee's name and address without verifying whether the assignee is a legally registered entity. The USPTO should require assignees to provide a certificate of good standing or a state registration number from the jurisdiction of organization, and the USPTO should verify this registration before recording the assignment. This reform would prevent unregistered or ghost entities from appearing in the USPTO assignment database and would create a direct link between the patent record and the corporate record. The implementation would require interagency coordination between the USPTO and state Secretaries of State.

**Recommendation 22:** Improve third-party patent aggregator accuracy standards. Platforms such as Google Patents, Justia.com, idiyas.com, and others provide valuable public access to patent data but are susceptible to data corruption, misattribution, and indexing errors. The industry should adopt accuracy standards requiring: (a) regular synchronization with USPTO primary sources; (b) error reporting mechanisms for users; (c) correction protocols for identified misattributions; (d) transparency about data sources and update frequencies. The USPTO or the Department of Commerce could issue guidance or requirements under the Digital Millennium Copyright Act or Federal Trade Commission Act to enforce these standards. The implementation would be primarily industry-driven but could be supported by regulatory oversight.

**Recommendation 23:** Enhance forensic investigation standards for intellectual property valuation documents. The original audit plan purported to be a forensic investigation but failed to meet basic standards of source verification, citation, and methodological transparency. The forensic investigation industry, including organizations such as the Association of Certified Fraud Examiners (ACFE) and the National Association of Certified Valuators and Analysts (NACVA), should adopt standards specifically for IP valuation investigations. The standards should require: (a) primary source verification of all patent claims; (b) independent corporate registration verification; (c) disclosure of all data sources and search dates; (d) prohibition on meaningless technical jargon; (e) peer review or quality assurance for documents used in investment or transaction contexts. The implementation would be industry-driven through professional association rulemaking.

**Recommendation 24:** Create a public database of patent non-practicing entities and holding vehicles to increase market transparency. The current patent ecosystem lacks a centralized registry of NPEs, patent trolls, and ghost holding vehicles. Investors, licensees, and competitors must conduct ad hoc investigations to determine whether a patent holder is a legitimate manufacturer, a research institution, or an enforcement vehicle. A public database, maintained by the USPTO or a private nonprofit, could track: (a) patent assignment history; (b) litigation and licensing activity; (c) corporate registration status; (d) beneficial ownership disclosures; (e) NPE classification based on activity patterns. The database would reduce information asymmetry and prevent fraudulent audits from inflating the perceived value of patents held by opaque entities. The implementation would require legislative authorization and funding, or a public-private partnership model similar to the OpenOwnership registry.

## APPENDIX E: GLOSSARY OF TERMS

**ACH (Analysis of Competing Hypotheses):** A structured analytical methodology that evaluates multiple hypotheses against the same evidence to determine which is most consistent with the facts.

**Beneficial Owner:** The natural person who ultimately owns or controls a legal entity, regardless of nominee directors or layered ownership structures.

**BVLOS (Beyond Visual Line of Sight):** FAA regulatory term for drone operations where the pilot cannot see the aircraft directly.

**CFIUS (Committee on Foreign Investment in the United States):** U.S. interagency committee that reviews foreign investments for national security risks.

**eVTOL (electric Vertical Take-Off and Landing):** Aircraft that use electric propulsion to take off and land vertically, without runways.

**Ghost Entity:** A corporate entity that exists in minimal official records (typically one database) with no verifiable physical presence, employees, or operational activity.

**NPE (Non-Practicing Entity):** A company that holds patents but does not manufacture products or provide services based on those patents; often asserts patents for licensing revenue.

**Patent Troll:** A pejorative term for an NPE that aggressively asserts patents through litigation or licensing threats, often against operating companies.

**PEP (Politically Exposed Person):** An individual who holds or has held prominent public office, creating heightened money-laundering risk.

**Pro Se:** Representing oneself in legal proceedings without an attorney.

**ReBAC (Relational-Based Access Control):** A technical term referenced in the original audit; the investigation found no meaningful forensic application of this concept.

**SFAR (Special Federal Aviation Regulation):** An FAA regulatory instrument for specific aircraft categories; the powered-lift SFAR was issued late 2024.

**Shell Company:** A corporate entity with no significant assets or operations, often used as a vehicle for financial transactions, asset holding, or liability shielding.

**TIA (Type Inspection Authorization):** An FAA certification milestone allowing flight testing for type certification.

**UAM (Urban Air Mobility):** The emerging industry for passenger and cargo transport within cities using eVTOL aircraft.

**UAV (Unmanned Aerial Vehicle):** A drone or remotely piloted aircraft.

**VTOL (Vertical Take-Off and Landing):** Aircraft capable of taking off and landing vertically without runways.

**UBO (Ultimate Beneficial Owner):** The final natural person who owns or controls a corporate entity, stripping away all intermediary layers.
